Found 2026-07-20 during the RV2-DEBT-059 design consult (independently confirmed by direct code read plus a second AI trace that reached the identical conclusion without seeing the first). Not reproduced with a minimal failing test yet; the mechanism is confirmed by code inspection (`rt_async_poll.c`, `run_ready_one`'s two switches vs. `apply_poll_outcome`'s scope-deferral branch), not by an observed failure. Not fixed here — out of scope for RV2-DEBT-059/062, which only needed the abandoned-state-box and nested-handle drop paths respectively; this is a scheduling-correctness gap in a different function entirely. | Open | Single-worker scheduling correctness (`rt_async_poll.c`, `run_ready_one`) — the natural fix is routing `run_ready_one`'s outcomes through `apply_poll_outcome()` like every other poller, but that's a design decision for whoever picks this up, not assumed here | A minimal repro proves a parent-with-active-children completes before a child in single-worker mode today; `run_ready_one` routes `POLL_DONE_CANCELLED` (and ideally all outcomes, for symmetry with every other poller) through `apply_poll_outcome()`; the repro is fixed and gated. |
| RV2-DEBT-064 | SOUNDNESS, pre-existing, newly found 2026-07-20 while building RV2-DEBT-059/062's non-copy remote-select e2e coverage (Epic 21 Task 8): `emitChannelSelectCrossing` (`internal/backend/llvm/emit_crossing_select.go`) has exactly ONE call site to `rt_far_channel_select`, shared between the true first attempt and every retry — unlike `emitChannelCreateCrossing`, which deliberately splits into separate `initBB`/`retryBB` blocks specifically so capacity/drop-id are computed ONCE (the retry branch passes literal `i64 0, i64 0, i64 0` instead of re-evaluating anything). Because a remote select is a crossing like any other, it needs at least one suspend/resume round trip to the arms' owner shard even at `SURGE_SHARDS=1` (matches every other crossing's self-crossing-still-uses-transport behavior); the compiled retry re-enters the SAME MIR block containing the whole arm-building loop, so a SEND arm's owned payload expression (`ch1.send(own job1)`) gets RE-EVALUATED on every retry — confirmed directly in emitted LLVM IR: the `build()`-style payload constructor was called twice per retry round (4 total calls across 2 rounds) feeding a `rt_far_channel_select` call whose OWN retry branch (`runtime/native/rt_far_channel_select.c`, `*pending != NULL`) never reads `send_bits`/`send_drop_fn_ids` at all — so every retry's freshly-built payload is silently discarded (leaked), and the FINAL, successful call's arm array holds whichever build happened to run last. Reproduces as a deterministic crash, not just a leak: `free(): double free detected in tcache 2` on a minimal two-arm non-copy select (`internal/vm/runtime_v2_far_channel_noncopy_e2e_test.go`'s abandoned `TestRuntimeV2FarChannelSelectNonCopyWinnerConsumedOnce`, removed from that file pending this fix rather than left permanently red). Confirmed NOT caused by RV2-DEBT-059/062's own new mechanism: the round-trip send/recv row (create + one send + one recv, no select) is clean under valgrind (only the pre-existing baseline residual noted in that same test file); this row's `record_select_commit`/`select_committed_index` write-once-read-once tracking was independently verified correct via direct instrumentation (traced winner/loser indices and per-arm drop-fn-id/bits at the free site — the skip/drop decision was exactly right every time) before this root cause was found — the crash is upstream of that mechanism, in the crossing's own retry plumbing, not in it. Distinct from RV2-DEBT-048 (CLOSED): that row's per-arm move-semantics fix (SEM3140/3141) is for LOCAL selects, whose winner is decided synchronously in one function; this row is specifically about the REMOTE crossing's async retry reconstructing owned arm values it should only ever build once. CLOSED 2026-07-23, and the mechanism above is CORRECTED — the crash is real and reproduced, but the retry re-evaluation this row blamed for it does NOT reproduce. ROOT CAUSE (valgrind, both frees and their order captured): DOUBLE OWNERSHIP of a losing SEND arm's payload on the normal, non-cancelled completion path. The runtime drops every non-committed arm in `rt_remote_task_pending_release`'s `select_arms` loop, reached on that path via `rt_far_channel_select` -> `rt_immediate_on_finish_retry` -> `..._consume` -> `..._release`; compiled code then drops the SAME pointer again, because sema's per-arm drop synthesis (`internal/sema/type_expr_select.go` ~92-121 — the moved-set union plus `oneSidedDroppables`, shared verbatim with `compare` and with LOCAL select, no far-specific branch) places a drop of every other arm's moved payload into the winning arm's block. FIX: hand the arm table's payload ownership back to the caller exactly when a winner reply reaches compiled code — new `select_finish_retry`/`select_disown_arms` (`runtime/native/rt_far_channel_select.c`) zero every arm's `payload_drop_fn_id` when the terminal reply is a genuine winner, so the free path drops nothing there; every other outcome (Cancelled reply, failed status, teardown that never reaches the caller) keeps the obligation on the free path unchanged. This mirrors the established clear-on-consume idiom for `result_drop_fn_id` (`rt_remote_task_api.c`'s `finish_retry`) but is GATED where that one is unconditional: a non-success result leaves `result_bits` at 0 so there is nothing to take, whereas an arm payload stays fully alive on a Cancelled reply AND the compiled cancelled path re-enters the pend edge without running any arm block. Gate correctness verified by direct read: `winnerBB` is the only path in `emit_crossing_select.go` reaching the arm dispatch (cancelled goes to the pend edge, every error status panics), and the arm-drop union is arm-count-general, so the caller reclaims ALL losers for any N. THE RETRY RE-EVALUATION IS ALSO REAL, but far narrower than this row stated, and it took an adversarial review to find the shape that proves it. Three first probes — (1) a send arm whose Copy value comes from a marker-printing function; (2) the same through a select with NO ready arm, so it genuinely parks owner-side and resumes through a real retry round; (3) a lease-minting arm RECEIVER (`b.share().recv()`) — ALL printed one marker and measured byte-identical `definitely lost` with and without an `initBB`/`retryBB` split, so the split was briefly (and wrongly) reverted as unfalsifiable. The probe set had a hole: those are all PLACE operands, which `splitAsyncAwaits` (`internal/mir/async_normalize.go`) temps into a preceding block, so a resumed retry only re-loads them. A CONST operand is embedded in the crossing instruction itself and lowers INLINE inside the crossing block — and several const kinds allocate there: an `int`/`uint`/`float` literal outside the fixnum inline range (`rt_bigint_from_literal` and friends, `emit_term.go`) and a string const (`rt_string_from_bytes`). Measured A/B against HEAD on a bignum-literal send arm: `72 bytes in 2 blocks` without the split versus `36 bytes in 1 block` with it, leak stack through `rt_bigint_from_literal` — exactly one orphaned bigint per retry round. The split is therefore KEPT, with the accurate mechanism recorded in its code comment so the next reader does not re-derive the place-operand probe and revert it again. Boundary condition for anyone touching that row: the literal MUST exceed the fixnum inline range — re-measured with a small literal the leak is 0 both ways, so an obvious-looking `send(7)` arm proves nothing. `emitChannelCreateCrossing`'s split is the same category, conditional on operand form here rather than unconditional there. PROOF ROWS: `TestRuntimeV2FarSelectConstArmEvaluatedOnce` pins the split (one un-reclaimed bigint, not two; its single remaining block is the separate pre-existing `typeOwnsHeap`-false-for-bignum gap, stated in the row and not fixed here). `TestRuntimeV2FarSelectNonCopySendArm` (`internal/vm/runtime_v2_far_select_noncopy_e2e_test.go`) — three far channels, one winning SEND arm buffered then reclaimed at channel teardown, one losing SEND arm reclaimed by the winning arm's compiled drop, exit-code rows at `SURGE_SHARDS=1,2,8` plus a valgrind row at STRICT ZERO definitely-lost; reverting the runtime fix reproduces the double free (negative control). Deliberately avoids `on ch {...}` so RV2-DEBT-061's intermittent race cannot be mistaken for this row's subject. HARNESS NOTE recorded during this work: the exit-code rows are NOT the real gate — glibc's tcache double-free detector is probabilistic, and the pre-fix binary passed them outright on some runs while valgrind reported the invalid free on that same binary. TWO ADJACENT FINDS, both fixed here: (a) the LLVM collect pass never collected `Crossing.RemoteOps[k].Value` (`internal/backend/llvm/emit_collect.go`), though the local `InstrSelect` case collects `arm.Value` right beside it — so a far-select SEND arm carrying any non-trivial constant failed to COMPILE (`missing string const`); (b) `runtime-v2-transport-check` was RED at HEAD (`db8e10a4`): that commit added `rt_far_channel_select`'s `send_drop_fn_ids` parameter without updating the C harness fixture in `internal/vm/runtime_v2_remote_publication_test.go`, so five rows failed to build — a broken gate hiding regressions, repaired by passing `NULL` for the new parameter. | Closed (double-ownership gate + const-operand retry split) | Epic 21 Task 8 select fork | A non-Copy far select reclaims every SEND arm payload exactly once — winner delivered, losers dropped once — proven valgrind-clean at strict zero, with the fix's negative control reproducing the double free; the existing Copy-payload select e2e/census rows rerun unchanged as regression guards. |
| RV2-DEBT-061 | SOUNDNESS, pre-existing, newly found 2026-07-20 while building RV2-DEBT-060's e2e coverage: `on ch { ... }` (the anchored/immediate-on channel-operation surface) carries an intermittent, RARE (roughly 10-25% of runs across two independent repros) invalid-free / invalid-write under valgrind on the LLVM backend, in the immediate-on retry/completion path — NOT in any far-channel handle or object code. Two stack shapes seen: (a) `rt_free` called from a compiled `drop.typeN` (the crossing state's own composite drop glue) invoked via `__surge_drop_call` from `rt_remote_task_pending_release` -> `rt_remote_task_pending_consume` -> `rt_immediate_on_finish_retry` -> `rt_immediate_on_execute_anchored`, freeing a block already freed elsewhere ("0 bytes inside a block of size N free'd"); (b) a size-1 invalid write inside `rt_immediate_on_dispatch_execute` (`rt_immediate_on.c:271`, called from `rt_remote_task_dispatch_message` -> `rt_remote_spawn_drain_inbound_locked` -> `rt_worker_main` on a DIFFERENT thread) landing inside a block `rt_remote_task_pending_release` (`rt_remote_task_pending.c:123`) had just freed — looks like a worker-thread write racing a pending's refcounted release on another thread. CONFIRMED PRE-EXISTING AND UNRELATED to RV2-DEBT-060/061's own work: reproduces on unmodified pre-Epic-21 HEAD (commit 7e56a513) at a similar rate (2/8 manual runs) with a program that only spawns tasks doing `on ch { ch.send(...)/ch.recv(...) }` in a loop — no far-channel handle-drop or channel-object-free code involved at all in the crash traces. Confirmed NOT caused by either the handle-drop fix (RV2-DEBT-060, `emitInstrDrop`'s far-channel case) or an earlier abandoned attempt to also drop far-channel composite fields via `emitDropValue` (removed after this investigation — see RV2-DEBT-060's closure note) — both variants showed the identical intermittent rate, matching clean HEAD. Repro not yet reduced to a minimal, deterministic form; the two programs used during investigation both mix real crossing traffic (send/recv through `on ch {...}`) with repeated `.share()`, at 4 iterations, under `valgrind --leak-check=full`. RV2-DEBT-060's own gate test (`TestRuntimeV2DropFarChannelHandleAndObjectValgrindZero`) deliberately avoids `on ch {...}` (create+share+drop only) specifically to stay race-free and not flake on this bug. ROOT-CAUSE LEAD (2026-07-20, found by codex during the Epic 21 Task 6 design review, lead-verified by direct read — a lead, not a confirmed fix): the publication-accepted handoff write `pending->state_owned = 0` (`rt_immediate_on.c` — this is shape (b)'s own crash-site line, ~271, already cited above) executes AFTER `state->lock` is released (unlocked a few lines earlier); it is a plain, unsynchronized write. `rt_remote_task_pending_release`'s free path reads `pending->state_owned`/`body_state` with no lock at all. A caller-side release racing the owner's handoff around this window has no synchronization between the two: a stale `state_owned==1` read during the race window would double-drop `body_state` via `__surge_drop_call` (matches shape (a) exactly), and the unsynchronized write itself can land in memory a concurrent release just freed (matches shape (b) exactly, same line the original crash evidence already pointed to). Not fixed here — Epic 21 Task 6 needs 053b/059 proven independently of this race, not entangled with fixing it. Candidate fix direction for whoever picks this up: move the `state_owned = 0` transfer under `state->lock`, in the same re-check window the surrounding code (`~242-254`) already uses for the analogous status check. PARTIAL PROGRESS 2026-07-23, row stays OPEN. A code-confirmed CONTRACT VIOLATION was found and repaired at the two sites this row's stack traces name, but the intermittent signature itself was NOT reproduced, so the repair is unproven against it. The publication-accepted handoff contract (`rt_remote_spawn_internal.h`) states its own safety invariant explicitly: "No path sees both releases because the dispatch lane still holds a pending reference when it clears the flag: the plain store is ordered before that reference's acq_rel refcount drop." The SPAWN family satisfies that by construction — its reference only leaves with the ACK, enqueued after the store. `rt_immediate_on_dispatch_execute` and `rt_far_channel_dispatch_select` do NOT: both hand the in-flight reference to the owner registration BEFORE `rt_remote_spawn_publish_body_task`, and publishing makes the body runnable, so another thread can complete it, consume that registration, drop the last reference, and free the pending before `pending->state_owned = 0` executes. That is exactly this row's failure shape (b) — a size-1 invalid write at `rt_immediate_on.c:271` landing in a block `rt_remote_task_pending_release` had just freed — and shape (a) is the same window read from the other side (a stale `state_owned` read double-dropping `body_state`). FIX APPLIED: both dispatches now take an explicit pending reference before publication and release it after the store, restoring the invariant the contract already claims. NOT PROVEN: a probe built to this row's own recipe — `on ch` send/recv through `.share()`d sibling leases, four iterations, under valgrind — did not reproduce the race in 50 baseline runs across `SURGE_SHARDS/THREADS` of 2/2, 2/4, 4/8 and 8/8, versus this row's recorded 10-25%, so the probe shape does not reach the window and cannot discriminate fixed from unfixed. The repair is kept because it is a four-line restoration of a documented invariant that the code demonstrably breaks, not because it was measured. SYNC POINT ADDED 2026-07-23, AND THE HYPOTHESIS DID NOT HOLD. `SP_IMMEDIATE_ON_AFTER_PUBLISH` now exists (allowlisted in `rt_sync_point.h` and `check_sync_points.sh`) exactly where this row's analysis said the window is: between `rt_remote_spawn_publish_body_task` and the `state_owned` store. It IS reachable — a harness row parked the dispatch there and the reached-count fired. But the free-inside-the-window shape did NOT reproduce, in either of two attempts: with the dispatch held at that point, (1) letting the published body complete and the caller resolve, and (2) additionally cancelling the caller inside the window to force the teardown sweep, both left the pending's reference count above zero, so the free path never ran there and no drop occurred. Decisive detail: BOTH attempts behaved identically with the repair applied and with it reverted, so the row was a test that could not fail and was REMOVED rather than kept as a false proof; the sync point itself is kept, since it is the correct window and is now available to whoever picks this up. CONSEQUENCE FOR THE REPAIR'S STANDING: it restores an invariant the handoff contract explicitly states and the code demonstrably broke, and that justification is unchanged — but two independent attempts now fail to show the invariant's violation is REACHABLE, so the repair should not be described as fixing this row's observed corruption. It is entirely possible the observed intermittent invalid-free has a different cause than the publication window, and the next investigation should treat the diagnosis as unconfirmed rather than as a lead already banked. What is now ruled out, and worth not repeating: draining the other references while parked at the window, with and without a forced caller cancel. | Open | Immediate-on / anchored crossing correctness (rt_immediate_on.c, rt_remote_task_pending.c) — needs its own root-cause investigation, not row-sized inside Epic 21 Task 7 | A minimal, reliably-reproducing repro exists (deterministic sync-point forcing, per this codebase's established technique, rather than a probabilistic loop); the race is root-caused (state-drop double-free vs. a genuine cross-thread write-after-free in the retry/dispatch path — the two stack shapes may be one root cause or two, and the root-cause lead above suggests they may be the SAME cause); a fix lands with a negative control; a gate row exercises `on ch {...}` under valgrind without flaking. |
| RV2-DEBT-092 | The mono dump lists every core generic instance TWICE: `--emit-mono` on `testdata/golden/mir/compare_guard_await_release.sg` prints 8 type rows for 4 distinct types (`Task::<bool>`, `Task::<int>`, `TaskResult::<bool>`, `TaskResult::<int>`), each pair carrying the SAME resulting `type#`. Found 2026-07-30 while root-causing the RV2-DEBT-070 remainder, and the two share a mechanism: one copy is keyed on core's own declaration symbol (`OrigSym` 63/64, low and stable) and the other on the SYNTHETIC per-file symbol that `injectCoreExports` mints for the same export (`internal/symbols/resolve_module_members.go`, `syntheticSymbolForExport`). Monomorphization keys instances by symbol, so one generic reached through two symbols instantiates twice. Both copies agree on the TypeID, so this is duplicated WORK and a confusing dump rather than a known miscompile — but it doubles the mono type table for every core generic, and any pass that counts or walks instances sees each one twice. NOT a determinism defect: the ordering was fixed with RV2-DEBT-070 and the duplicate rows are now stable. Deliberately NOT fixed there, because deduplicating means changing how mono keys instances, which reaches codegen and deserves its own measurement. | Open | Mono instance keying | One generic reached through both its declaration symbol and a synthetic import symbol produces ONE mono instance, shown by the dump listing 4 rows for the 4 distinct types. |
| RV2-DEBT-093 | SOUNDNESS, shipped and live for one day: a `compare` arm whose RESULT is one of its own payload bindings both handed the payload onward AND freed it. `compare v { Payload(s) => s; Empty() => ""; }` — the ordinary spelling — aborts with `free(): double free detected` on the native backend when the result is a fresh allocation, and reads freed memory (3 memcheck errors) when the caller looks at the value first. Introduced by `a8351f82`, whose parent `2df134e1` is clean, bisected on a 12-line reproducer. That commit gave an arm's payload bindings the scope-exit obligation they never had, correctly, and reasoned that `liveDroppables` would skip a binding the arm moved onward — but NOTHING marked that move. `observeMove` is called on a block's TAIL expression and on a `return` operand; an arm's result is the same consuming position and no walk reached it, so the binding stayed live in the arm's drop scope and earned a drop it had already given away. An arm that uses its payload LOCALLY is unaffected, which is why the commit's own measurement looked right. FIX: observe the move on the arm's result, skipped when the compare's value is DISCARDED — there the result goes nowhere, nobody downstream owns it, and the binding's obligation is exactly what reclaims it; an independent review confirmed that guard and found no other missing move on the compare path (guards reject arm-binding moves already, `finally` arms and nested compares go through the same arm-result path). WHY IT REACHED main WITH A GREEN GATE: two existing e2e rows (`TestRuntimeV2CompareScrutineeReleaseCensusBalanced`, `TestRuntimeV2DropUnionCastReclamation`) DID catch it and were red on the tree, but `make test` sets `SURGE_SKIP_TIMEOUT_TESTS=1` and every native e2e test skips on it — see RV2-DEBT-094. GATE: `TestCompareArmReturningItsPayloadOwesNoDrop` (`internal/sema`) asserts the returning arm owes no obligation while a locally-using control arm owes exactly one; it is in a package `make check` actually runs, costs milliseconds, and its negative control is reverting the `observeMove`, which fails it with 2 obligations instead of 1. Measured: reproducer goes from abort to `in use at exit: 0 bytes in 0 blocks` with 0 memcheck errors, and both e2e rows go green. FIRST FIX WAS WRONG AND A REVIEW CAUGHT IT: observing the move at the ARM decides a question only the ARM'S CONTEXT can answer. `let out = compare v { Payload(s) => s; ... }` consumes the result, but `peek(compare v { ... })` with a `&string` parameter only BORROWS it — there nobody downstream owns the payload, and taking the obligation away leaked one string per evaluation (measured: 16 blocks over 16 iterations, and clean with the move reverted). The `!compareDiscarded` guard covered the discarded case and missed the borrowed one, which is the same mistake one step along. CORRECTED SHAPE: the arm KEEPS the obligation by default — the leak-over-double-free direction, while the answer is unknown — and the consuming context takes it back, via `releaseArmResultObligations` called from `observeMove`, which is exactly the set of positions that consume a value. Nested compares recurse; an arm whose result is a BLOCK needs nothing, because a block's tail already observes its own move while the arm is typed. Verified across all five contexts at strict zero: consumed by `let`, borrowed by a call, discarded, used locally inside the arm, and a nested compare. The gate grew a third arm for the borrowed shape, and its negative control now reports 3 obligations instead of 2. | Closed | Compare arm result move | Done: an arm that returns its payload binding neither leaks it nor frees it twice, pinned by a sema gate that runs in `make check`. |
| RV2-DEBT-094 | The native e2e corpus does not gate a commit, in TWO independent ways. First, `make test` runs `SURGE_SKIP_TIMEOUT_TESTS=1 go test ./...`, and every LLVM e2e test calls `ensureLLVMToolchain` -> `skipTimeoutTests`, which skips on any non-zero value of that variable — so `internal/vm` reports `ok` in `make check` having run none of its native suite, including every reclamation census. Second, even run directly the package exceeds `go test`'s default 10-minute timeout (measured 619s to completion), so a plain `go test ./internal/vm/` reports a TRUNCATED failure set that looks like a regression and is not; `-timeout 40m` is required. Not theoretical: RV2-DEBT-093 shipped a double free with `make check` green, and the two rows that would have caught it had been red on the tree since. A third trap sits next to these: the e2e tests exec `./surge`, so rebuilding the binary while the suite runs produces spurious failures indistinguishable from real ones. ALSO measured: the `TestLLVMParity` subtest set that fails VARIES run to run (13 subtests on one tree, 22 on the same tree with a change stashed, the smaller a strict subset of the larger), so the parity rows are themselves flaky under parallel load and cannot be read as a signal without a same-session baseline. Consequence: `make check` is a gate for compile-time behavior and says nothing about memory safety, while reading as if it covers both. Deliberately NOT fixed on discovery — the suite takes minutes and making it unconditional is a CI-shape decision, not a code fix. The owner has flagged leak-hunting tooling as a thing to design properly later rather than now, and this row is the concrete instance to design against. | Open | Runtime gate coverage | A memory-safety regression in `compare`, drop or crossing lowering cannot reach a commit with a green gate — either the native e2e corpus runs in the commit gate, or a fast proxy for it does. |
| RV2-DEBT-095 | SOUNDNESS, pre-existing: a TERNARY moves a value into its result without anyone observing the move, so a binding handed to `cond ? a : b` is both consumed and dropped. Measured 2026-07-30 on a 16-line reproducer: `let picked: string = i < 2 ? a : b;` gives 8 memcheck errors in 4 contexts — `Invalid read of size 8` and `Invalid free() / delete / realloc()`, i.e. use-after-free AND double free. Confirmed pre-existing: identical on `2df134e1`, before the compare-arm work of RV2-DEBT-093. Same mechanism as that row and found by an independent review of its fix: `typeExprTernary` (`internal/sema/type_expr_values.go`) types both branches and calls `consumeTempCandidate`, but never `observeMove`, so the `let` bindings it consumed keep the scope-exit obligation they gave away. The review names the select arm result (`internal/sema/type_expr_select.go`) as structurally identical; that one is NOT yet measured and should be before it is claimed. WHY THE FIX IS NOT ONE LINE: only ONE branch runs, so observing the move on both would mark both bindings moved and LEAK whichever branch was not taken. `compare` already solves exactly this with per-arm moved-set snapshots plus `oneSidedObligations`, which drops a binding live on this path and moved on another; a ternary has none of that machinery. So the correct fix is to give the ternary (and select) the same per-branch treatment, not to add the missing call. A naive one-line fix would trade a double free for a leak — an improvement under this codebase's leak-over-wrong-free rule, and still not the answer. CLOSED 2026-07-30, and the scope grew twice while measuring. FIRST: the same hole exists in `compare` for a binding declared OUTSIDE it — `compare c { true => a; _ => ""; }` where `a` is an enclosing `let` was 16 memcheck errors, which RV2-DEBT-093 did not cover because that row was about an arm's OWN payload binding. SECOND: a nested ternary written the way people write one, `x ? (y ? a : b) : c`, went unrecognised because the branch is a GROUP expression and the payload accessor does not see through parentheses. FIX: a ternary and a compare are the SAME shape for this — several branches, exactly one runs, and whichever it is hands its result outward — so both go through one `consumeBranchResults`, called from `observeMove` like the RV2-DEBT-093 retraction, because only the consuming context knows whether the value is consumed at all. Each branch is walked through `observeMove` (so a nested choice settles itself first and this one reads the result), the union of the branch moved-sets becomes the state after the expression, and each branch is given `oneSidedObligations` against what the OTHER branches moved — the existing compare-arm channel, landing on `ArmDropsExpr`, which `wrapArmDrops` already applies to any expression. That is what stops the fix from trading the double free for a leak of the untaken branch. Groups are peeled by `unwrapGroups`. Verified at strict zero across: ternary with two bindings, one binding and a literal, fresh call operands, borrowed result, nested in the true branch, nested in the false branch, nested on both sides, a ternary inside a compare arm, a compare inside a ternary branch, a compare arm returning an outer binding, two arms returning different outer bindings, and the RV2-DEBT-093 payload shapes consumed and borrowed. GATE: `TestTernaryBranchesMoveAndCompensate` (`internal/sema`) asserts BOTH halves — each of the two consumed branches owes exactly one compensating drop — so it fails on a fix that moves nothing (0 obligations, the pre-fix state, double free) and equally on one that moves both and compensates neither (0 obligations, leak). Its negative control reports 0 across 0. Goldens unchanged. A REVIEW OF THE FIX FOUND A THIRD SHAPE, which measurement confirmed and which is now fixed too: a branch can move something while it is TYPED — `cond ? pass(a) : b` hands `a` to a by-value parameter — and the branches were typed without isolating their moved-sets, so the second inherited the first's moves and nothing noticed `a` was still live on the path where `pass` never ran. `typeExprTernary` now snapshots and restores around each branch and records the same one-sided compensation, which is what `compare` has always done across its arms. Measured on that shape: 17 memcheck errors in 3 contexts at the parent commit (a double free AND a leak) -> 0. NOT fixed here and filed as RV2-DEBT-096: a compare or ternary whose result is only BORROWED leaks what the taken branch produced — measured identical before this work where it overlaps, so it is older and has a different cause. | Closed | Branch result move | A value moved into a ternary or select result is dropped exactly once on every path, pinned by a memcheck-clean reproducer and a sema-level obligation assertion. |
| RV2-DEBT-096 | A `compare` whose result is only BORROWED leaks a binding an arm returned from OUTSIDE the compare. Measured 2026-07-30: `peek(compare i < 8 { true => a; _ => ""; })` with `peek(x: &string)` and `a` an enclosing `let` loses 136 bytes in 8 blocks over 16 iterations — exactly the 8 iterations that take the arm returning `a`. Pre-existing and NOT caused by the branch-move work of RV2-DEBT-095: byte-identical with that change stashed, so the cause is older and separate. Note the shapes AROUND it are clean, which is what makes it narrow: the same compare CONSUMED is clean, the same borrow of an arm's OWN payload binding is clean, and a borrowed ternary is clean. So something on the borrow path consumes the binding without giving the value an owner, rather than the arm-result treatment being wrong — the RV2-DEBT-095 machinery deliberately does nothing here, because a borrowed result moves nothing. Likely neighbours to read first: `consumeTempCandidate` and the implicit-borrow path (`internal/sema/implicit_borrow.go`), which calls `observeMove` itself. ALSO SEEN on a TERNARY, which widens the row beyond compare: `peek(cond ? pass(a) : b)` leaks 8 blocks over 16 iterations — exactly the 8 that take the `pass(a)` branch, i.e. the borrowed TEMPORARY the taken branch produced, not the binding. RV2-DEBT-095's branch isolation halved this one (16 blocks -> 8) by reclaiming the untaken side, so what remains is purely the borrowed result. That both spellings leak only the value the TAKEN branch produced, and only when the result is borrowed, is the shared symptom to explain. ROOT-CAUSED AND PARTLY FIXED 2026-07-30, and the symptom description above was too narrow: it is not about BORROWING, it is about the choice expression's value having no owner at all. Discarding it leaks the same way. MECHANISM, and it was written down as a deliberate bound: `noteTempCandidate` (`internal/sema/temp_drops.go`) flags evaluations that PRODUCE an owned value so a statement-end drop reclaims them, and control-flow expressions were excluded wholesale — "their value can forward a PLACE from any arm... fresh values built in arms leak when the outer value is discarded — the safe direction, recorded as a bound". Both halves of that are true, which is why the rule cannot simply be reversed: a branch that forwards a place must NOT be released (use-after-free through its owner), and a branch that built its value must be. So the question is asked PER BRANCH: a choice expression owns its value only when EVERY branch minted one. FIX: `branchMintsItsValue` + `choiceOwnsItsValue`, consulted from `noteTempCandidate` for `ExprTernary`/`ExprCompare`, asked BEFORE the branch candidacies are consumed because consuming is what erases the evidence. SOUNDNESS POINT FROM REVIEW, which changed the predicate: being a pending temp candidate is necessary and NOT sufficient — a CAST is flagged yet reads its source without consuming, and an identity cast lowers to the same pointer, so `c ? (a to float) : (b to float)` would look like two fresh values while both alias a live binding. The kind must say the value was MINTED, so the test admits call / aggregate literal / string literal / non-assigning binary and excludes cast, unary and index, each of which has a forwarding spelling. Measured: the all-branches-mint shapes go from 16 leaked blocks per 16 evaluations to strict zero, borrowed and discarded alike; the forwarding controls and the consumed regressions stay at zero. A SECOND REVIEW ROUND found the rule too narrow in one direction: a branch that is itself a choice ALREADY PROVEN to mint on every branch has the same standing as a call, and rejecting it by kind left `a ? (b ? make() : make()) : (c ? make() : make())` unflagged — measured 16 leaked blocks, now 0. Inner choices are settled before the outer asks, so the answer is simply looked up. GATE: `TestChoiceOwnsItsValueOnlyWhenEveryBranchMints` (`internal/sema`) pins the rule from every side — never flagging reports 0 and leaks, always flagging reports 2 more than it should and double-frees, dropping the nested case reports 1 short, and only the per-branch rule with nesting reports the expected 2. It carries a nested FORWARDING shape too, so "recurse into nested choices" cannot be read as "always recurse". MIXED, DISCARDED CASE FIXED 2026-07-30, and it needed no new mechanism: a discarded choice has no consumer to hand the candidacy to, so each branch simply KEEPS its own and frees what it built at its own region's end — where it was materialized, so the drop is dominated. The forwarding branch was never a candidate and keeps forwarding. `cond ? a : build()` as a statement goes to strict zero. STRUCTURAL FINDING that bounds this: the same trick does NOT work for a discarded `compare`, and trying it REGRESSED an already-clean shape. A compare is normalized into a block whose arms deliver by `ret`, and `flushTempDropsForRet` deliberately KEEPS the value an exit delivers rather than freeing it — so an arm's own temp survives the arm. Ternary and compare therefore need different treatments here, which was not visible before measuring. BORROWED MIXED TERNARY FIXED 2026-07-30, and NOT by the continuation pushing the review recommended — a cheaper design turned up while scoping that one. A BOOLEAN GUARD: `owns = false` before the choice, raised by the branches that BUILT the value, and the release emitted under it. Strictly better than duplicating the consumer into the branches, which would also reorder side effects (`f(g(), cond ? .. : ..)` moves `g()` inside a branch), and better than the null-sentinel design, which the VM blocks by refusing to drop an uninitialized slot. The guard needs nothing new: the choice's result is assigned on EVERY path already — only who owns it varies, which is exactly what the guard records — and it rides the same `TempDrops` entry, so a context that turns out to CONSUME the value removes both at once and cannot leave a guarded drop behind. NESTED CASE, and it took two rounds: a nested choice does not count as minting for its parent — claiming it raises the parent's guard on the paths where the inner FORWARDED, a double free — and it must not earn a release of its own either, because that fires at the end of the enclosing BRANCH, before the join copies the result, which is a read of freed storage. The answer is that the guard PROPAGATES INWARD: the parent owns the value, and the inner's own minting branches raise the parent's guard, since they are the only ones that know something was built. A review found the second half; my own matrix missed it because its conditions VARIED, so the faulty path was not always reached — the review's used constants. Constant-condition variants are now part of the shape matrix. A THIRD round found the rule was still binary where the question is TERNARY: a branch either mints on every path, mints SOMETIMES (it is a guarded choice), or forwards. A yes/no test answers "no" for the middle case, so a choice with such a branch on BOTH sides owned nothing at all while both inner values had already been taken as branch values — every building path leaked (`peek(p ? (q ? build() : a) : (r ? build() : a))`, 8 blocks over 8 iterations). Sometimes -minting now makes the enclosing choice own AND guard without raising the guard itself. TWO SELF-INFLICTED DEFECTS, both the SAME trap and both caught by measurement: `NoLocalID` is -1 while a zero value is 0, a REAL local — first as an uninitialised struct field (a bool stored into a string slot), then as a `tempDropEntry` literal elsewhere that did not name the new field (an `if` on a pointer, which broke three native rows that `make check` and the valgrind matrix both passed). Fixed the second time by removing the trap rather than the instance: a separate `guarded bool`, so the zero value means no guard. Measured: 10 shapes at strict zero — both orientations of borrowed mixed, nested mixed inside mixed, and seven controls. GATE: the ownership test now pins THREE outcomes (unconditional / guarded / none) with two negative controls — never guarding leaks, and claiming a guarded nested choice double-frees. MIXED `compare` CLOSED TOO, and the `ret` delivery is what shaped the fix: a compare is normalized into a block whose arms deliver by `ret`, so there is no branch body left for the lowering to annotate by position the way a ternary's is. The arm therefore SAYS SO in the tree — `ExprRaiseReleaseGuard` wraps a minting arm's result, evaluates to its operand and raises the guard — which reuses the ternary's mechanism unchanged. Both mixed compare shapes, borrowed and discarded, go to strict zero, as do the cross-construct nestings (a ternary in a compare arm, a compare in a ternary branch). SEPARATE defect uncovered here and filed as RV2-DEBT-098: an arm returning its OWN PAYLOAD binding frees it while the result is still borrowed — byte-identical before this work, and hidden all session by a borrow probe that never dereferenced. The review's recommended answer is path-sensitive lowering — push the consumer's continuation into each arm, so a fresh arm builds, uses and drops in place while a forwarding arm just uses — which fixes mixed without cloning and respects the arm-frame invariant. A blanket "choice results are owned" rule was considered and rejected: it needs a clone rather than a retain for a forwarded `string`, changes `let x = c ? a : b` from moving to cloning, and cannot be implemented at all for a move-only resource. | Closed | Borrowed compare result | Done: a choice expression releases what its branches BUILT, on exactly the paths that built it, for both ternary and compare and in every context. |
| RV2-DEBT-097 | SOUNDNESS, pre-existing: an IDENTITY cast produces an ALIAS while being classified as a freshly produced value, so releasing its result frees storage a live binding still holds. Measured 2026-07-30: `let x: float = ...; let y: float = ...; cond ? (x to float) : (y to float);` as a discarded statement, then reading `x` and `y`, reports `Invalid read of size 4` — byte-identical with the RV2-DEBT-096 work stashed, so it predates it. MECHANISM: `noteTempCandidate` (`internal/sema/temp_drops.go`) flags every `ast.ExprCast` as producing an owned value, but `lowerCastExpr` reads its source with `consume=false`, and a cast whose source and target types agree lowers to the same pointer. So the flag says "fresh value nobody owns" about something a binding owns. Found while root-causing RV2-DEBT-096, by an independent review that offered it as a counterexample to a predicate based on the temp-candidate flag alone; the counterexample as first written does not compile (borrowing a temporary is rejected earlier), and the DISCARDED spelling above does, and reproduces. RV2-DEBT-096's fix deliberately excludes `ExprCast` from the kinds it trusts, so it neither depends on nor worsens this — but the flag itself is still wrong for a cast in every OTHER consumer of it. DISCUSSED AND DECIDED 2026-07-30, so this is ready to work. MEASUREMENT FIRST, which settled the fork: the CONSUMED form is broken too (`let b = a to float`, then reading `a`), and the sema flag does not govern that — consuming removes it. So the defect is in the LOWERING, not the classification. `lowerCastExpr` (`internal/mir/lower_expr_ops.go`) reads its source with `consume=false`, an ALIAS, and then calls `markOwningTemp` UNCONDITIONALLY — while the comment two lines above it says the marking is for a cast that CHANGES REPRESENTATION. The code does not match its own stated intent, and the missing condition is the bug. Measured across contexts: identity cast discarded, consumed by a `let`, and as a call argument all report `Invalid read`; a WIDENING cast (`int` to `float`) and no cast at all are clean. DECISION (owner): fix at the lowering — mark the temp as owning only when the cast actually changes representation. Explicitly REJECTED the alternative of making the intrinsic honour the call contract with a retain/clone, even though `to` is designed as a MAGIC METHOD (`a to T` lowers to `a.__to(target)`; intrinsic for built-ins, `extern<Foo> { fn __to(...) }` for user types) and that alternative would have kept one semantics for both. The owner's reasoning is a general principle: it is FINE for primitives and the operations on them to be cheaper at runtime than user types, and a user `let b = a to Bar` will be cheap enough with good optimization. THE ASYMMETRY MUST BE DOCUMENTED — that is part of the fix, not an afterthought. Note the fix is NOT "never mark a cast temp as owning": for a user `__to` the marking is correct, since that is a real call returning an owned value. The predicate is whether the lowering actually produced a fresh value. Identity-ness is decidable in sema: `to` on a type PARAMETER is rejected today (`SEM3015`), so it cannot become identity only after monomorphization. DELIBERATELY OUT OF SCOPE, both by the owner's decision: (1) an "unnecessary conversion" diagnostic — it looked obvious until the user-`__to` case came up, since `extern<Foo> { fn __to(self: Foo, target: Foo) -> Foo }` should arguably be banned yet might carry real normalization logic, and the owner will return to that question themselves; (2) constant overflow on `to` (`max_value::<int32>() to int8` crashes) — accepted behaviour and a future ENHANCEMENT, explicitly not debt. ALSO SEEN and to be resolved with the fix: the MIR for a discarded identity cast frees the same temp TWICE (`drop L3` twice), which may be a consequence of the alias or an independent defect of the temp channel. FIXED 2026-07-31 (commit `575f075d`, with the doubled statement-temporary release in `12d67483`). MEASUREMENT REFINED the decided mechanism: `markOwningTemp` is NOT what released the alias. Every temp holding a reference-counted scalar is registered for a release at `newTemp`, the single point where temps are born, so the cast temp owed a reference the moment it existed while `lowerCastExpr` filled it with a bare alias — which is why the decided predicate (own it only when the cast CHANGES REPRESENTATION) was right about the rule and off by one mechanism. The fix applies the same rule one level up: a cast whose source and target resolve to the same type mints no temp and emits no cast at all, and reads its operand with the CALLER's intent, so `a to float` lowers exactly like `a`. Sema agrees, which the close condition demanded: an identity cast is no longer a producer, and `unwrapTempCandidate` sees THROUGH it so consumption reaches the operand that really produced the value. That second half closed a shape nobody had measured — `let x = 1.5 to float` left the literal's candidacy unconsumed and the statement's release freed the block the binding had just taken; broken identically at the parent commit, so it was older than this work. Rows measured 0 memcheck errors after, red before: discarded, `let`-bound, argument, literal-bound, literal-discarded, literal-through-a-ternary, and a 16-iteration loop; controls (widening `int to float`, no cast at all) unchanged. Gates: `TestRuntimeV2IdentityCastKeepsItsSourceOwned` (native, valgrind, strict-zero leaks) and `TestIdentityCastSurvivesTheHeapSanitizer` (interpreter, which REFUSES a released slot instead of nulling it — the native backend cannot see a release too many). The doubled `drop` was a second registration, not an alias effect: `lowerOwnedTempExpr` registered its temp explicitly on top of the automatic one, so `2.5;` as a discarded statement panicked on the interpreter. Documented asymmetry: `docs/LANGUAGE.md` §6.6. NOT fixed here and filed as RV2-DEBT-099: a borrowed `&float` is released by the operations that read it. | Closed | Cast produces an alias | A discarded or borrowed identity cast is memcheck-clean, and the cast's temp-candidate classification agrees with what its lowering actually emits. |
| RV2-DEBT-098 | SOUNDNESS, pre-existing: a `compare` arm that returns its OWN PAYLOAD binding frees it while the caller is still BORROWING the result. Measured 2026-07-30: `peek(compare mk(i) { Payload(s) => s; Empty() => ""; })` with `peek(x: &string)` that actually READS its argument reports `Invalid read of size 8`, 16 errors over 16 iterations — byte-identical before and after the RV2-DEBT-096 work, so it is older and independent. MECHANISM: RV2-DEBT-093 gave an arm's payload bindings a scope-exit obligation, and `releaseArmResultObligations` retracts it when the compare's value is CONSUMED. A BORROWED result is not consumed, so the obligation stands and rides the arm's `ret` as `DropsAfterValue` — the payload is freed right after the arm delivers it, and the borrower then reads it. The retraction is correct for what it was written for; what is missing is that a borrow keeps the value alive past the arm. HOW IT HID: my own probe for borrowed shapes was `fn peek(x: &string) -> int { return 1; }`, which never dereferences, so every measurement of this shape read clean for a whole session. A borrow probe MUST read the borrowed value — that is the methodological fix, and the matrix now uses `len(x)`. FIXED 2026-07-31 (commit `abd33b34`). The row's mechanism held exactly. The fix is TWO halves that must land together, and the row's own history says why: an earlier attempt dropped the obligation alone and leaked one string per evaluation, which is what the retraction-on-consumption design was working around. (1) An arm whose result IS one of its own payload bindings no longer owes that binding a release — it answered with it. (2) That arm now counts as MINTING the compare's value, so the compare owns the result and releases it when the statement ends, which is the owner the leak was missing. Answered from the arm's OBLIGATIONS rather than its pattern, which keeps a borrowed subject out of it: a payload bound from `compare *slot` earns no obligation and so is never handed out. Measured 0 memcheck errors and 0 definitely-lost, red before: borrowed (16 invalid reads → 0), consumed, discarded, returned across a function boundary, and an arm that BUILDS from its payload (`Payload(s) => s + "!"`), where the payload stays the arm's and the built value is the compare's. MIR proof: the Payload arm's `drop` is gone and the release now sits after the borrower's call. Gates: `TestRuntimeV2ComparePayloadOutlivesItsArm` (native, valgrind, strict-zero leaks), `TestComparePayloadSurvivesTheHeapSanitizer` (interpreter), and the sema assertion `TestCompareArmReturningItsPayloadOwesNoDrop`, which was UPDATED: it pinned the old borrowed-arm obligation, and now pins both halves — one arm obligation (the payload that never leaves its arm) and exactly one statement-end release (the borrowed compare's own value). NOT fixed here and filed as RV2-DEBT-100: a compare over a BORROWED subject takes its payload by move. | Closed | Borrowed compare payload | A compare arm's payload binding outlives a borrow of the compare's result, pinned by a probe that dereferences. |
| RV2-DEBT-099 | SOUNDNESS, pre-existing: reading a BORROWED reference-counted scalar releases the value the borrow points at, so the owner's block is freed while the borrow is still live. Measured 2026-07-31 at `71fab2dd` and byte-identical after the identity-cast work, so it is older and has a different cause: `fn peek(v: &float) -> int { if v > 0.0 { return 1; } return 0; }` called on a live `float` binding reports 22 memcheck errors in 17 contexts, and `fn peek(v: &float) -> float { return v to float; }` reports 53 in 36. The shape that is CLEAN is the one that binds first (`let c = *v;`), which is the tell: MIR mints a deref temp for the pointee (`L2 = (* copy L0)`), every temp of a reference-counted scalar is registered for a release when it is born, and the deref filled it with an ALIAS — the same contract violation as the identity cast, in the other operation that forwards storage it does not own. `retainExtractedValue` is the existing repair (a field read, an element read and a borrowed union payload all take it); a deref through a REFERENCE wants it too, and a deref of an `own` pointer deliberately does not, since there the single reference transfers. Found while measuring RV2-DEBT-097 — the first borrow probe written for that row read every shape as broken, including the controls, because the probe itself was the defect. Costs more than it looks: it makes `&float` unusable in any function that READS it, which is most of them. FIXED 2026-07-31 (commit `a5a9e795`). The row's diagnosis held and was HALF the contract; the other half only became visible once the first was fixed. (1) The deref temp now takes a reference through a REFERENCE operand (`retainExtractedValue`, `derefsABorrow`), and a deref of an owning pointer deliberately still transfers. (2) That alone traded the use-after-free for a LEAK in one shape, `let c = *v;`, which had looked clean only because two defects cancelled: sema marks a binding initialized by a projection read as an ALIAS that never drops, which is right for a value living inside its container and wrong for a reference-counted scalar — a copy at the surface, a counted block underneath. The alias marking now excludes that type (`projectionReadAliasesItsSource`, applied at both the `let` and the reassignment site), so the binding is a real owner. Half (2) closed a leak with NO borrow in it at all: `let f = b.value` on a `float` field leaked 24 bytes per evaluation (192 bytes over 8 iterations), measured before any of this work. Rows now 0 memcheck errors and 0 definitely-lost, red before: compare, arithmetic, `to string`, a forwarded borrow, a deref into a by-value parameter, a bound deref (used and returned), a field read in a loop, and 8 repeated borrows. Gates: `TestRuntimeV2BorrowedScalarSurvivesItsReaders` (native, valgrind, strict-zero leaks — the leak column is asserted BECAUSE a use-after-free fix can trade into it) and `TestBorrowedScalarSurvivesTheHeapSanitizer` (interpreter). The rows use `float` deliberately: an `int` outside the inline range leaks on its own under RV2-DEBT-035, which would drown the measurement. | Closed | Borrowed scalar released by its reader | A borrowed `float` can be compared, cast and printed inside a callee with the caller's binding still live, memcheck-clean, and the deref temp's ownership agrees with what the reference contract says it holds. |
| RV2-DEBT-100 | SOUNDNESS, pre-existing: a `compare` over a BORROWED subject treats the subject as its own, so the arm frees a payload the caller still owns. Measured 2026-07-31 at `71fab2dd` and byte-identical after the RV2-DEBT-097/098/099 work, so it predates all of it: `fn look(slot: &Slot) -> int { return compare *slot { Payload(s) => peek(&s); Empty() => 0; }; }` called on a live `Slot` binding reports 32 errors in 2 contexts — an invalid read AND an invalid free. MECHANISM, from the MIR: `compare *slot` derefs the borrow into a temp (`L3 = (* copy L0)`) and MOVES it into the compare's subject (`L2 = move L3`), so the subject slot holds the caller's union box while claiming to own it; the arm then takes the payload by move (`L5 = move L6`) and releases it at the arm's end (`drop L5`), freeing storage the caller's binding still points at. Same family as RV2-DEBT-099 — a deref temp filled with an alias — but for a COMPOSITE, where the repair is not a retain: a borrowed subject's payloads must be read as borrows, and the arm must own nothing it did not build. Found while gating RV2-DEBT-098; deliberately left OUT of that fix, whose predicate is obligation-based and therefore correctly does nothing here. FIXED 2026-07-31 (commit `ef3f30eb`). The row's MIR trace named the subject move; the defect that actually reached user code was the PAYLOAD half of the same question, and it is far from exotic: `format("hello {}", fmt_arg(name))` reported an invalid read AND an invalid free on every call with a string argument, measured identically at `71fab2dd` (RV2-DEBT-039 closed the earlier double-free on this same user-facing path by a different mechanism; this is a second, independent one). MECHANISM: sema registered every arm payload binding as a droppable, so a `string` payload read out of a BORROWED union owed a release, and the union belongs to whoever the borrow points at. The comment on `registerComparePayloadDroppables` claimed a borrowed extraction retains — true only of reference-counted scalars, which is exactly what `TagPayloadData.SubjectBorrowed` exists for. FIX: the obligation is skipped when the subject is only read, with three exceptions that still own what they took — a refcounted-scalar payload (retains), a `@copy` union (CLONED into the compare even through a deref), and a subject that is not a union at all (MOVED into its binding; a review caught this one, one leaked string per evaluation, and it is the single place the sema classification deliberately parts from HIR's `compareScrutineeOwnership`, which asks about the envelope box rather than the bindings). Rows measured 0 invalid ops and 0 definitely-lost, red before: a borrowed payload read, a borrowed payload appended to a string, a `@copy` union through a deref, a borrowed `float` payload, a plain non-union subject, an owned subject. Gates: `TestRuntimeV2BorrowedComparePayloadStaysTheOwners` (native, valgrind) and `TestBorrowedComparePayloadSurvivesTheHeapSanitizer` (interpreter); the existing scrutinee-release census covers the `@copy` row from the other side. NOT fixed here: the SUBJECT move this row's title names is left as written — it is harmless once the payloads stay the owner's, since the subject temp of a borrowed union is not released. Two neighbours measured and left open: a borrowed TUPLE compare (`compare *(&pair) { (left, right) => ... }`) double-frees, filed as RV2-DEBT-101; and `format`'s own slice/range temporaries still leak 376 bytes per 8 calls, which is RV2-DEBT-039's recorded residual under RV2-DEBT-040. | Closed | Borrowed compare subject | A compare over a `&Union` can bind and read payloads with the caller's value still live, memcheck-clean, and the subject temp's ownership agrees with the borrow it was read through. |
| RV2-DEBT-101 | SOUNDNESS, pre-existing: a `compare` over a BORROWED TUPLE binds its elements as owned, so the arm frees storage the tuple still holds. Measured 2026-07-31, byte-identical at `71fab2dd` and after the RV2-DEBT-100 fix (6 memcheck errors, ending in `free(): double free detected in tcache 2`): `let pair: (string, string) = ("left", "right"); compare *(&pair) { (left, right) => len(&left) to int + (len(&right) to int); }`. Same family as RV2-DEBT-100 and deliberately NOT covered by it: 100's predicate answers only for UNION subjects, because a non-union subject is normally MOVED into its binding and taking its obligation away leaks. A tuple pattern is the third case — the subject is borrowed AND the pattern binds PARTS of it, which is the field-read family (`markAliasedBinding` territory) rather than the payload-extraction one. Found by an independent review probing the RV2-DEBT-100 fix for over-reach; it reported the classification as correct and left this shape as evidence. CLOSED 2026-08-03 by integrated commits `b33d1db4` and `adaa1c37`: root tuple decomposition through a borrow no longer registers element drops, while an alias-aware bounded type walk preserves `@copy` through alias chains before deciding the borrowed case. Sema/HIR/MIR controls report borrowed `0`, owned `2`, direct/one-alias/two-alias `@copy` `2`, non-Copy alias `0`, and a value handed onward `1`; VM is green and LLVM/Valgrind is strict `0/0`. The first independent review caught the alias-resolution `@copy` gap; the follow-up fixed it and independent re-review returned APPROVE. | Closed | Borrowed tuple compare | Done: the borrowed/owned/Copy/non-Copy/handoff ownership matrix is pinned in sema, HIR and MIR, the executable VM probe passes, and LLVM/Valgrind is strict `0/0`. |
| RV2-DEBT-102 | SOUNDNESS, pre-existing, found by Epic 25's full-corpus ownership verifier: a heap-backed optional copied from a borrowed recursive field can remain an alias while a loop carries the binding into a later consuming `compare`. The original `recursive_handles.sg` spelling used `let mut id: NodeId? = start`, then `id = *n.next` and consumed `id` on the next iteration; MIR gave the loop-carried binding an aliasing definition and a later release, so the verifier correctly refused to certify it. Epic 25 did not change the compiler for this non-blocking general case: the sema-only fixture was equivalently rewritten to consume `start` once, carry the scalar `NodeId`, and borrow `*n.next` only as the current compare subject. That removes the unsafe dependency without hiding a corpus finding. | Open | Post-Epic-25 loop ownership / drop-lowering follow-up | A minimal executable loop proves the current alias/release failure on both backends; borrowed heap-backed projections are cloned/retained or rejected before entering a loop-carried consuming binding; the original optional-carry shape is verifier-clean and memcheck-clean without a fixture rewrite. |
| RV2-DEBT-089 | ownership-compile-failure:CF-010. The partial-move gate decided ownership from an UNRESOLVED type and got it backwards. Whether a field read takes its value or duplicates it is decided by asking whether the type is Copy, and `isCopyType` answers false for a type it does not know — so a read whose type never got recorded looked exactly like a move of a move-only value. Member access on an ANONYMOUS record records no type at that point, which is how `let p = { x: 1 }; let a = p.x;` came to be reported as a partial move of an `int`, and `l.__opaque` on a BORROWED extern struct the same way. Introduced 2026-07-28 by Epic 24 step 1 (`12e8dac6`), found 2026-07-29 by chasing why `make golden-check` was red. FIXED: the projection gate is skipped when the read's type is unknown; the whole-binding path is untouched, since it needs no type to know a binding went. The underlying gap — member access on an anonymous record produces no recorded type — is NOT fixed and is a separate question: nothing else consulted that type, so nothing else misread it, but the next rule that does will hit the same thing. Golden rows restored: `sema/valid/user_record_type`, and the `entropy`/`net` false positives in the corpus dump. | Fixed for the gate; the missing type on anonymous-record member access is open | Partial-move gate reading an unresolved type (sema) | Done: a Copy field read of an anonymous record is accepted, and the gate refuses to answer from a type it does not know. Open: anonymous-record member access records its type, so the next consumer does not have to guard. |

The row's own framing (records "differ only in `ModulePath`") is exact, and explains why only builtin RECEIVERS conflict: a nominal type ingested twice gets two type identities, so its two records dispatch on different receivers and were never rivals, while a builtin type has one program-wide identity that both records claim. A builtin declaration carries no body; it names an operation the compiler implements, so however many modules spell it out there is one operation to select. Records fold only when they agree on receiver, signature and attributes — the exact agreement `sameBuiltinOperation` required — so the 123 records whose types genuinely differ between ingestions all survive, and a user declaration is never builtin. `collapseBuiltinOperations` and `sameBuiltinOperation` are deleted. | RV2-DEBT-151 | NONBLOCKING interval measure taken deliberately during Wave C. Ordinary composites move to inline exact-layout storage in Wave C, but the transport boundaries that carry them — channel send/receive, task result publication, and select staging — are Wave D and Wave E work and are not ready to receive an inline value. For the length of that interval a value crossing a transport boundary is copied into a fresh transport allocation on send and adopted, copied out, or freed on receive, through exactly ONE named helper set per backend. The set has THREE operations, not two: copy in at send, adopt or copy out at receive, and RELEASE an unconsumed payload. The third is not an implementation detail of the other two — after the cutover the generated drop glue drops a composite's contents and frees nothing, because there is no longer a box to free, so without a dedicated release entry point every refused, cancelled, or never-received payload leaks its transport allocation. On the VM side there is no far payload path at all to separate: `types.KindFar` appears once in production (`internal/vm/storage_shape.go:63`) and `internal/asyncrt` has no shard or crossing concept, so a far channel send IS the ordinary send. **A separate obligation the deletion must not absorb:** the RELEASE leg (`requireRuntimeOwnedRelease`) has a second job beyond payloads — it also serves abandoned suspension frames (`emit_async.go:112` and `:149`), for which `hasInlineStorage` is false by design because a frame's address is handed to the runtime at suspend and read back at resume. Retiring the payload helpers therefore does not retire the release entry point; that survives until every owner has typed storage of its own. |

| RV2-DEBT-158 | LATENT BLOCKER-CLASS: the native map hands out a raw interior address that its own insert and remove invalidate, and nothing refuses the shape. `rt_map_get_ref` (`runtime/native/rt_map.c:189`) returns `*out_bits = (uint64_t)(uintptr_t)&map->entries[idx].value` — an interior pointer into the entry buffer — and `rt_map_get_mut` (`:200-201`) is literally `return rt_map_get_ref(...)`, so a SHARED borrow and a MUTABLE borrow of a map value are the same call returning the same writable address. Both invalidation events are live: `map_ensure_capacity` reallocs `map->entries` on growth, and `rt_map_remove` is swap-with-last. Sema does NOT refuse an insert across a live `get_mut` borrow — verified: a program holding `Some(p)` from `m.get_mut(&"a")` and performing five inserts before `*p = 99` passes diagnose with zero diagnostics. The VM is safe by CONSTRUCTION and not by check: a VM map-element `Location` resolves by key through `MapInfo`, not by address, so the same program runs correctly and prints 99. The native path has no such protection. It is latent only because RV2-DEBT-157 blocks native map index assignment, so the reproducer cannot currently be built natively — closing 157 makes this reachable. Pairs with RV2-DEBT-154: a shared borrow yielding a writable interior pointer, while sema refuses no store through a shared reference. **CORRECTION 2026-08-09: this row's claim that "the VM is safe BY CONSTRUCTION (map elements resolve by KEY through `MapInfo`, never by address)" is FALSE — see RV2-DEBT-172 for the mechanism and the code.** The VM is immune to address invalidation and not to index invalidation, and its own `remove` performs the latter. Do not plan against the safety claim; Wave D's plan relies on it three times. **OWNER RULING 2026-08-09: option A — sema refuses container mutation across a live element borrow**, the same answer on both backends, so the native map is fixed by making the shape unreachable rather than by making the address survive. | Open | Epic 23b, container lane; ruling taken, decided together with RV2-DEBT-172 | A native reproducer is built and shown to write through a stale address under Valgrind (or the shape is proven unreachable, with the argument recorded); then either sema refuses container mutation across a live element borrow, or the runtime stops handing out addresses that its own operations invalidate. Under inline storage the same question must be answered for a composite value rather than a word. VM/native parity rows cover get_mut-then-insert, get_mut-then-remove, get_ref-then-insert, and the growth-reallocation boundary. |

| RV2-DEBT-181 | An intermittent witness race in `TestRuntimeV2FarTaskCallerCancel/cancel_after_publication_before_first_poll/shards_2`: the row fails with `missing far-body witness at shards=2` under full-suite load and passes 5/5 in isolation, so it is a scheduling race rather than a defect in what it measures. | RV2-DEBT-180 | BLOCKER-CLASS: a native `timeout()` cannot protect a network wait, because the runtime advances its clock past the wait. `advance_time_to_next_timer` (`runtime/native/rt_worker_turn.c:250`, `:273`, `:288`) jumps the virtual clock straight to the next timer deadline whenever the executor samples idle, and a task parked on network readiness does not hold that sample back — so any `timeout(task, ms)` wrapping a net wait resolves Cancelled almost immediately, whatever `ms` says. Measured: the stdlib HTTP server built with `accept_timeout_ms = 60000` exits 0 after `elapsed=0.10` seconds — a sixty-second timeout served in a tenth of a second. Each accept worker wraps `net.accept` in `timeout(...)` (`stdlib/http/accept.sg:29`), takes the Cancelled arm (`:44-47`), stops, and once all four workers stop `serve` closes the listener and returns (`stdlib/http/server.sg:453-476`). `strace` confirms bind/listen succeed and each worker gets exactly one `accept(5) = -1 EAGAIN` before `exit_group(0)`. The control is decisive: with `accept_timeout_ms = 0` — the branch that arms no timer at all — the server stays up indefinitely and parks on accept correctly, so the park path is sound and only the clock is wrong. WHY THIS WENT UNSEEN FOR SO LONG: `TestMTCorrectnessHTTPServer` IS the gate, and it has been failing; the client reports `read: connection reset by peer` / `EOF`, which reads like a network flake, so the row was recorded as intermittent rather than as a server that exits before answering. A virtual clock that ignores a class of parked task is the same defect shape as a gate that is green without running: the timer is measuring something other than what the program is waiting for. | Open | Runtime V2 scheduler clock | A net wait protected by `timeout(task, ms)` survives at least `ms` of wall clock before it is cancelled: the idle sample does not advance past a deadline while any task is parked on external readiness. Proven by the HTTP server accepting and answering a request with a non-zero `accept_timeout_ms`, and by a direct row asserting that a timeout over a socket that never becomes readable fires no earlier than its deadline in real time. `TestMTCorrectnessHTTPServer` passing is a necessary consequence, not the evidence. |
| RV2-DEBT-179 | A suspension frame does not record whether it owns what it holds, so which reclamation to use is a convention with two consumers and no check. Two emitter sites hand a frame away and need OPPOSITE treatment. The yield that finds its task already cancelled abandons a frame the pend block has just packed, so the frame is the only owner of the locals inside it and reclamation must WALK them (`internal/backend/llvm/emit_async.go:154` registers the walking release). The cancel terminator holds a payload that was read out at the top of the poll into locals that have since been released, so walking it would free those values a SECOND time (`emit_async.go:725-745` emits the shallow one inline). Nothing in the frame's type, its layout or its MIR distinguishes the two: the native backend does not act on `Field.MoveOut` at all — the identifier appears nowhere under `internal/backend/llvm` — so reading a payload out leaves the frame's bytes exactly as they were. Both function comments state the rule, which is why it is correct today. But a THIRD site that abandons a frame — a new crossing form, a scope-join variant, a blocking body — would pick a release by guessing, and guessing the walking one on a spent frame is a double free rather than a leak. No gate catches the difference: the strongest witness in the tree is one Valgrind row, and a leak is what a wrong choice produces only in the harmless direction. Found 2026-08-09 by the reviewer of the leak-lane patch, which is what made the convention visible. | Open | Epic 23b Wave D (async frame ownership) | The frame itself answers who owns its contents, rather than the caller remembering: either a spent frame is marked when its payload is read out, or the two releases become one that can tell. Proven by a negative row — a frame abandoned through the wrong path is REFUSED at build time or trapped at run time, not silently double-freed — plus the existing Valgrind witnesses staying green. |
| RV2-DEBT-178 | A captured reference into an async poll activation outlives the activation, and the VM refuses the read instead of running the program. The VM keeps a task's state as a composite — a reference to bytes in some activation's arena — rather than as a counted object. An arena carries a generation and retiring one bumps it, so every reference taken beforehand is refused afterwards (`internal/vm/storage_value.go`, `StorageRef.resolve`). Exactly one path defends against this: `execTermAsyncYield` calls `collectTaskStatePins` before `dropFrameLocals` (`internal/vm/vm_terminator.go:103`), which pins the arena and retains what the extent holds, and retirement is then refused while a pin is outstanding (`internal/vm/task_state_pins.go:256-271`). Something in the HTTP accept worker holds a reference the pin walk does not reach, so its activation retires underneath it and the next read kills the server mid-request: `panic VM1999: storage: stale reference to type#601`. This is the pin walk being incomplete rather than the generation check being wrong — the check is the thing that turned a use-after-free into a diagnosable panic, and it should stay. Note this row and RV2-DEBT-180 both surface through the same HTTP fixture from opposite backends: the VM refuses a stale read while the native side exits early on a clock that outran the wait. | Open | Runtime V2 VM task-state pinning | The pin walk reaches every reference a parked task can still read, so no activation retires while one is outstanding. Proven by the HTTP server fixture completing a request/response cycle on the VM, and by a targeted row that captures a reference into a poll activation across a yield and reads it after — passing rather than panicking. |
| RV2-DEBT-177 | The VM cannot iterate or range-index a FIXED array, so `for x in a` and `a[[i..j]]` over `T[N]` are LLVM-only shapes that panic at run time rather than being refused. `evalIterInit` (`internal/vm/eval_iter.go:35-54`) switches on the iterable's value kind and accepts only `VKHandleArray` and `VKHandleRange`; under ordinary storage a fixed array is a composite with no handle, so a loop head panics `VM1003: expected iterable, got composite` (reproduced on a five-line `int[3]` loop). A range index never reaches a slice path at all: `evalIndex` (`internal/vm/eval_data.go:73`) now routes a fixed array to `evalStorageIndex` (`:491`), which takes an integer and nothing else, so the range falls through to `arrayIndexFromValue` and is rejected at `internal/vm/array_utils.go:114` with `expected int, got range`. Before the VM's composite-storage flip a fixed array was a heap `OKArray` object and both paths worked; the flip removed the handle these paths dispatch on. The gap is not one missing branch — a VM array view is an `OKArraySlice` whose base must be an `OKArray`, so there is nothing for a fixed array's slice to point at. Sema accepts both shapes as readily as for dynamic arrays (`internal/sema/type_array_view.go:83-99` falls back to `arrayFixedInfo`), so nothing refuses them earlier. WHY NO GATE CAUGHT IT: the only fixed-array-slice gate is an IR-shape assertion (`internal/backend/llvm/emit_array_range_test.go:27-44`) that checks the emitted IR contains a call and never runs the program; the differential row that would have caught the VM half, `TestLLVMParity/array_range_indexing`, was already red because the NATIVE half segfaulted on the same lines — with both halves broken the mismatch named neither. The native half is fixed now, so that row is a live VM-only failure. **CLOSED 2026-08-10.** A fixed array in ordinary storage now has both. The VM had two element addressing models — a heap array object whose elements are whole Values, and an arena extent whose elements are layout-encoded bytes — and no single view that could be either, which is why every range and slice path dispatched on a heap handle that 75b56918 had taken away. `arrayView` now carries either base and five accessors are the only code that knows which, so a rename of its object field made the Go compiler enumerate all 23 readers rather than leaving one to dereference nil. A slice over a fixed array names the array's own extent, so writing through the slice lands in the bytes the base reads: the ref IS the alias. A range needs no arena awareness at all, because `for x in f` and `f.__range()` first take a slice header over the extent and then iterate that. One trap was load-bearing and worth recording: `for x in f` lowers to `iter_init copy L0`, and a copying operand DUPLICATES a composite into scratch, so an iterator built the obvious way would name an extent that the deferred drop releases and the instruction-boundary rewind invalidates — the next `iter_next` would read a stale reference. `evalIterInit` therefore resolves the iterable through `EvalPlace` and owns nothing, the same way a move-out field access does. Verified: `arrays_slice_view` prints its aliased write, `array_range_panics` prints all four rows, and `for x in f` over `int[3]` sums correctly. NOTE for KNOWN_LIMITATIONS, recorded there: a slice or range that OUTLIVES the fixed array it names is now refused by the VM with a stale-reference panic, where the native backend silently dangles — sema accepts the shape on both. The VM being stricter here is an improvement, not a divergence to paper over. | Closed | Runtime V2 VM container storage | A fixed array iterates and range-indexes on the VM, or the language refuses both shapes at sema with a diagnostic naming the edit — a run-time `VM1003` is neither. `TestLLVMParity/array_range_indexing` green is the evidence, plus a row that iterates `T[N]` where `T` is a composite. |
| RV2-DEBT-176 | Native `rt_fs_read_dir` builds an array of DirEntry POINTERS where the language expects an array of inline DirEntry VALUES. `DirEntry` is `{ name: string, path: string, file_type: FileType }` (`core/intrinsics.sg:32-36`), which the layout registry lays out inline at 24 bytes, and the intrinsic is declared to return `Erring<DirEntry[], FsError>` (`core/intrinsics.sg:53`). The runtime builds `void** elems`, stores an 8-byte `DirEntry*` per entry (`runtime/native/rt_fs.c:536-556`), copies that pointer buffer into the array's data block (`:565-575`) and hands back a header over it (`:578-584`). The consumer walks by the element type's own stride, so entry 1 is read 24 bytes in — inside the neighbouring pointer words — and the `name`/`path` handles projected out are arbitrary addresses. This is one level deeper than the Metadata payload defect fixed alongside it: an array element rather than a union payload. Partially closed 2026-08-09 — `TestLLVMParity/fs_dir_smoke` now passes — but `TestLLVMParity/walkdir_for_in` remains red, so the element-buffer half is not fully retired. WHY NO GATE CAUGHT IT: the only two rows that read a directory are both under `TestLLVMParity`, which `make check` hides behind its timeout skip. | Open | Runtime V2 native fs intrinsics | A directory listing round-trips on both backends with every entry's `name` and `path` readable, `walkdir_for_in` green, and Valgrind clean over a directory with more than one entry. |
| RV2-DEBT-175 | A union whose live arm is a bare TYPE alternative frees nothing on the native backend. `Erring<T, E> = Success(T) | E` admits a value into the `E` arm by TYPE rather than by tag (`internal/vm/storage_union.go:149`, `wrapTypeArm`, names this shape).
