| RV2-DEBT-174 | **PARTIALLY CLOSED 2026-08-08.** The compiler half landed in `853aafba`: a coroutine writes its suspension state in place instead of rebuilding the two boxes on every entry to a pending block, so an aborted park now costs nothing. Re-measured afterwards at nine runs per row, **44 of 46 rows report a single value across all nine**, and the twenty-five budgets that had drifted were re-captured from those agreeing runs (the manifest schema takes an exact key set and its parser is itself a digest-frozen harness file, so the sample count backing each number is recorded here and in the commit message rather than as a new manifest field — that part of the close condition is deliberately unmet, not overlooked). **What remains is the whole of the gate-contract half, and exactly two rows:** `channel-unbuffered-scalar` (nine runs: 3×5, 4×4) and `channel-unbuffered-composite` (100×5, 99×3, 101×1). Their spread is ±1 and sign-flipping, which is NOT the ±2 aborted-park signature the compiler half removed — a second contributor, still unidentified, lives on the unbuffered-channel path. Until it is found or the contract gains a tolerance, those two rows cannot hold an exact budget and the canonical benchmark cannot go green, so their budgets are left at their stale values rather than set to a modal number that would make the gate a coin flip. ORIGINAL FINDING: the carrier benchmark's allocation counts are NOT DETERMINISTIC, and the gate asserts exact equality, so eleven rows could not pass at any threshold. Measured by the lead 2026-08-08, three samples per row on a quiet host: `far-immediate-scalar` [281, 283, 281], `far-select-scalar` [681, 679, 679], `blocking-scalar` [342, 344, 342], `blocking-composite` [408, 406, 406], `far-large-result` and `far-task-composite` [665, 667, 665], `far-channel-scalar` [672, 672, 674], `far-jumbo-contention` [1170, 1170, 1172], `channel-unbuffered-scalar` [131, 131, 133]. The spread is almost always exactly 2 and clusters on far/blocking/channel rows; purely local rows (`local-*`, `array-*`, map scalar rows) are perfectly stable. ROOT CAUSE, pinned by return-address attribution over 30 runs (16×281 with `spurious_wakes_absorbed=0`, 14×283 with `=1`, perfect correlation): a compiled coroutine's "pending" block heap-allocates exactly TWO boxes — the resume-payload union arm and the `{pc,payload}` state struct — before `rt_async_yield`, and frees and rebuilds them on every re-entry. When a wake token arrives inside `park_current`'s register-then-commit window the park is ABORTED and the task is requeued READY, so the coroutine re-polls, re-enters the same pending block, and rebuilds the pair: `allocation_count = floor + 2 × spurious_wakes_absorbed`. The counter itself was cleared of suspicion first, with an LD_PRELOAD interposer on malloc that independently counted 577 vs 579 real allocations for the 281 vs 283 runs. TWO CONSEQUENCES BEYOND THE GATE. (a) This is not a micro-optimisation: **128 of `far-immediate-scalar`'s 281 budgeted allocations are these per-park pairs** (64 parks × 2 in the inner coroutine, confirmed by site histogram), so removing them is a large allocation win on every async program, not just a determinism fix. (b) **The frozen budgets were captured from single runs**, so whichever interleaving happened at capture time became the contract: `channel-unbuffered-scalar`'s recorded 130 is a RETRY value while the quiet floor on the reference host is 131 (34 of 40 runs), which is why that row reads as "+1 over" rather than as a budget snapped to a rare schedule. WARNING for whoever fixes it: the obvious "give the coroutine an activation-lifetime slot" CANNOT be an alloca — the pending block ends in `unreachable` immediately after `rt_async_yield`, so the C frame is destroyed at suspension while the state pointer outlives it and the runtime may still call the abandoned-state drop through it. **OWNER RULING 2026-08-09: find the second contributor; do not hide it, and do not give the contract a tolerance.** The two remaining rows keep their stale budgets and the canonical benchmark stays red until the cause on the unbuffered-channel path is pinned the way the first one was — return-address attribution over ~30 runs plus an `LD_PRELOAD` malloc interposer to clear the counter of suspicion. A ±1 tolerance was considered and refused: it would blind the gate on exactly the two rows already known to be wrong. Scheduled as its own work item, not as part of the gate-integrity step. | Open | Epic 23b, async lowering; ruling taken, own work item | Two independent things, both settled. The compiler half: a pending block re-entered for the same state index stops freeing and rebuilding its two boxes — either by overwriting the existing pair in place, or by giving the coroutine one runtime-owned suspend-state allocation for the largest variant — so an aborted park costs zero allocations and the eleven rows become stable across repeated runs. The contract half: every budget is re-captured from at least three agreeing runs rather than one, and the manifest records how many samples backed each number, so a single rare interleaving can never again become a frozen requirement. Evidence is the same rows measured ≥5 times with a single distinct value each, plus `spurious_wakes_absorbed` observed non-zero during those runs to prove the aborted-park path was actually exercised and no longer costs anything. |
| RV2-DEBT-173 | NOTHING in the repository's automated gates executes a compiled Surge program, so no gate can observe a wrong runtime answer. Three independent facts, each verified 2026-08-08. (1) `make golden-check` regenerates FRONTEND sidecars only: `scripts/golden_update.sh:245` explicitly excludes `*.out`, `*.code`, `*.args`, `*.stdin` and `*.flags` from the regeneration sweep, so `.out` files are frozen inputs that are content-hashed but never re-derived. (2) The fifteen behavioural runners that DO execute `.out`/`.code` fixtures are all behind `//go:build golden` (`internal/vm/vm_async_golden_test.go:1` and fourteen siblings), and no target, script or CI job anywhere invokes `go test -tags golden` — grep over `Makefile`, `scripts/` and `.github/workflows/` returns zero hits. (3) CI's only behavioural step, `.github/workflows/ci.yml:181` → `scripts/run_testdata.sh --backend vm`, is a `# TEMPORARY STUB` (`:27-29`) that echoes `OK (stub)` and exits 0 unconditionally; 228 of that script's 279 lines are commented out. What `golden-check` genuinely proves is worth stating so it is not over-credited again: the frontend did not drift, and no lane silently reblessed a recorded output. It cannot prove behaviour, and Wave C's "golden-check ×2 with zero drift" was recorded in the wave's evidence as though it could — the cutover's actual behavioural evidence was the ordinary-storage proving corpus (15/15 on both harnesses, `known_red/` emptied) and the crossing gate, both of which do run programs. This row is the ninth instance of the pattern the wave has been recording all along: a gate reporting green while not exercising what its name claims. **REFINEMENT 2026-08-09, with a dated instance.** One clause above is too harsh on `golden-check` and the correction matters, because it locates the actual hole: `Check` (`internal/goldencheck/runner.go:53-69`) DOES re-run the generator and DOES `Diff` the regenerated corpus against the recorded one, so it is fully capable of seeing a frontend fixture drift away from what the compiler emits. What it cannot do is run: `make check` (`Makefile:278-285`) is `test` + `lint` + `c-check` + file sizes and never invokes it, so the only place it fires is CI, which is not gating this branch. The consequence was found the hard way while renumbering diagnostics: `testdata/golden/mir/compare_guard_await_release.mir` had been stale since `853aafba` (2026-08-08) changed a coroutine to write its suspension state in place — the fixture still recorded the old `L4 = struct_lit {__pc=..., __payload=...}` where the compiler now emits `L4.__pc = ...` / `L4.__payload = ...`, and `853aafba` touched no `.mir` fixture at all. Proven pre-existing rather than caused by the renumbering: the compiler rebuilt from `2d5bfb83` via `git archive` emits the new shape while that commit's own recorded fixture holds the old one. It was the only fixture in this state. So the gate that could have caught it exists and is correct; it is simply not wired to anything a commit has to pass. Whatever step 3 builds must be reachable from `make check`, not only from CI. **PARTIALLY CLOSED 2026-08-10.** Eleven of the fifteen runners now execute compiled Surge programs inside `make check`, on every commit, and `internal/vm` takes about 47s of its 90s package budget doing it. The tag turned out not to be a mode switch at all: `internal/vm/test_helpers_test.go` was `//go:build !golden` and defined helpers that some fifty unconstrained test files use, so setting the tag removed the helpers from files that still referenced them and the package did not COMPILE — the runners were not merely uninvoked, they were unrunnable. The cause was duplication: `repoRoot`, `envWithStdlib`, `buildSurgeBinary`, `runSurgeWithInput` and three package variables existed in two byte-identical copies, and the two tags were what kept them from colliding. Deleting the duplicates let the tag come off. Run for the first time, the runners failed 20 of 122 fixtures, all pre-existing; fourteen are closed (`inspect.go`'s three deleted bodies, the `Erring` envelope at `exit`, two unfinished halves of the fixed-array storage flip, the double drop of a refcounted scalar, a cancelled task denied a ready result, tuple types never interned structurally, and four recorded expectations that were wrong rather than the code). Four runners keep the tag, each with a comment naming its remaining red case and what that case needs, so the tag now marks known redness instead of hiding it. STILL OWED, and this row stays Open until all of it lands: the last six cases; the runners running on the NATIVE backend as well as the VM; `scripts/run_testdata.sh` un-stubbed or deleted with CI pointed at the real target; a clean-tree target for `TestGoldenUpdateDeterminism`, which cannot live in a pre-commit hook because its preflight demands a committed corpus and a hook runs on exactly the opposite; and the proof by breaking — change one recorded answer, watch the gate go red, restore it, watch it go green. **2026-08-10, second pass — everything in this row except the native lane being GREEN is now done.** The corpus runs on both backends: the thirteen near-identical copies of "run a recorded program and compare its output" are one `runBehaviourCorpus` taking the backend as a parameter, `make behaviour-check` is the VM lane the pre-commit hook already runs, and `make behaviour-check-all` adds the native lane. The native lane is a separate target by owner ruling rather than part of the hook, because it costs ~6s per fixture against the VM lane's ~0.26s — full tests at every important step, not at every commit. A requested lane whose toolchain is missing FAILS rather than skipping, since a silently skipped lane is the shape of gate this corpus replaces. `scripts/run_testdata.sh` is DELETED rather than un-stubbed — its commented-out body did what the Go runners now do, and a second implementation of the same comparison in bash only guarantees the two drift; CI runs the make targets instead, and the artifact-upload step that only the deleted script fed is gone. `make golden-corpus-determinism` gives `TestGoldenUpdateDeterminism` the clean-tree target it needs (471s, green) and CI runs it. **The proof by breaking is done and recorded here so it is not asked for again:** `TestVMTuplesGolden` green, `single=42` changed to `single=43` in the recorded `.out`, the gate went red naming the fixture, the line was restored, the gate went green. Also fixed on the way, and worth naming because the refactor is what exposed both: a `.flags` sidecar was silently dropped by the first draft of the shared helper (`async_fuzz_seed_123` runs the fuzzed scheduler), and the `_`-prefix convention that marks a recorded-but-not-run fixture was not honoured. WHAT REMAINS is the native lane going green, which is no longer this row's work: the VM lane has zero failures and the native lane's 25 are filed as RV2-DEBT-184 (eight the native backend will not compile), 185 (three wrong answers), 186 (a silent abnormal exit), 187 (panics without span or backtrace, owner ruling taken) and 188 (eight scheduler-order differences, owner decision required). This row closes when those five do. | Open | Runtime V2 gate integrity; native lane tracked by RV2-DEBT-184..188 | A gate that runs on every commit executes compiled Surge programs and compares their real output against the recorded `.out`, on BOTH backends, and fails when the output differs. Concretely: the `golden` build tag is either dropped or invoked by a target that `make check` reaches; `scripts/run_testdata.sh` is un-stubbed or deleted rather than left echoing success; and the new gate is proven by breaking it — change one recorded answer, watch the gate go red, restore it, watch it go green. Until that exists, no claim of the form "behaviour did not change" may cite `golden-check` as its evidence. |
| RV2-DEBT-172 | The VM's map element borrow is INDEX-KEYED, not key-keyed, so the VM has the same class of dangling-element bug RV2-DEBT-158 records for the native map — and 158's text asserts the opposite. That row states "The VM is safe BY CONSTRUCTION (map elements resolve by KEY through `MapInfo`, never by address)". It does not. `internal/vm/intrinsic_map.go:246` (`get_mut`, and `:172` for `get_ref`) builds `Location{Kind: LKMapElem, Handle: handle, Index: idx32}` — a POSITIONAL index resolved once at borrow time from `obj.MapIndex[key]`, with the key not retained. `internal/vm/place_container.go:221-224` (`storeMapElem`, and `:184-196` for `loadMapElem`) then resolves `obj.MapEntries[idx]` BY INDEX, bounds-checked only; `MapInfo` is consulted at `:225` solely to obtain the value TYPE for union retagging, never to re-resolve the key. And the VM's own removal is swap-with-last (`intrinsic_map.go:421-431`), identical in shape to native `rt_map.c:241-248`: it repairs `obj.MapIndex[swapKey]` but nothing repairs outstanding `Location`s. So `let p = m.get_mut(&"b"); m.remove(&"b"); *p = 99` writes into the slot `"c"` was swapped into, and `place_container.go:241` first calls `vm.dropValue` on the occupant — dropping `c`'s live value and then overwriting it. Memory-safe in Go's terms; an ownership bug in the language's terms, and silently wrong data either way. The distinction that matters: the VM is immune to ADDRESS invalidation, which is what 158 is about on the native side, and NOT immune to INDEX invalidation, which its own remove performs. 158's cited native reproducer (five inserts between `get_mut` and the write) does pass on the VM, because `append` does not shift indices — removal does, and nobody tried removal. Found 2026-08-08 while adversarially re-reviewing the Wave D plan, which relies on 158's VM-safety claim three times over: to scope 158 as native-only, to declare the container lane unblocked, and to reduce the VM's obligation to "keep key resolution honest" — a mechanism that does not exist. **OWNER RULING 2026-08-09: option A — sema refuses.** Mutating a container while a borrow of one of its elements is live is a compile error, on both backends, decided at the only stage that still knows the borrow is outstanding. The alternative considered and rejected was making the borrow survive relocation (a stable slot table or key retention on the native side, the key kept in `Location` on the VM side): it costs work on every element access and changes the container representation in the middle of the wave that is already changing it. Option A costs nothing at run time and removes the VM/native asymmetry at compile time, which is what the no-GC ownership model asks for. | Open | Epic 23b, container lane; ruling taken, implementation pending | The VM and the native map answer the element-borrow question the SAME way, whatever way is chosen, and 158's text is corrected so no future reader plans against a safety claim the code does not make. Whichever answer is taken — sema refuses container mutation across a live element borrow, or the borrow yields something that survives relocation — it is proven by a VM row and a native row that both perform `get_mut`, then a `remove` that relocates the borrowed entry, then a write through the old borrow, and both refuse or both behave identically. A test that only inserts does not close this row. |
| RV2-DEBT-171 | The channel's non-blocking receive core POPS A VALUE AND DISCARDS IT when the caller does not want the bits, and `select`'s readiness probe is exactly such a caller. `rt_channel_try_recv_status_owner_locked` (`runtime/native/rt_channel_sync.c:38-70`) calls `buf_pop(ch, &val)` UNCONDITIONALLY as its first act and only publishes the result under `if (out_bits != NULL)` at `:68`. `rt_async_select.c:318` polls arm readiness with `rt_channel_try_recv_status_locked(ex, handle, NULL)`, so a `SELECT_CHAN_RECV` arm that merely reports "ready" has already consumed the value the channel held, and nothing frees or re-queues it. This was invisible for as long as the discarded word was a scalar or an address nobody owned. It became measurable the moment the anchored send began publishing composite payloads into transport allocations: `far-select-composite` leaks 4,096 bytes in 64 blocks under Valgrind, exactly one 64-byte payload per operation. The leak is the mild reading. The severe one is that a readiness PROBE has a side effect on the thing it probes, which means the value is gone before any arm can bind it — and the reason no test noticed is that the far-select fixture's arms bind nothing, so its checksum is correct while its payload is silently destroyed. That also retires an inference recorded during Wave C: `far-select-composite` was NOT evidence that the anchored send was sound; it passed because its stale payload address was never adopted, a false negative. Found 2026-08-08 by the lead while closing RV2-DEBT-169. | Open | Runtime V2 channel/select core | A readiness probe does not consume. Either the recv core stops popping when it has nowhere to publish the value, or `select` stops asking the question through a mutating entry point — whichever keeps the shard-lock discipline intact, since every `channel_pop_candidate_locked` caller already holds the owner shard lock and the fix must not widen what runs under it. Proven by a `select` over a channel carrying a composite whose arm BINDS the payload: the value arrives intact, is dropped exactly once, and Valgrind reports zero bytes lost; plus a negative row showing that a probe which reports "not ready" has removed nothing. |
| RV2-DEBT-170 | BLOCKER, memory corruption in the integrated Wave C branch: a task result delivered TWICE is adopted twice, so the second delivery frees an allocation the first already freed. `task-clone-composite` aborts with `double free detected in tcache 2` on every run at `fb1f9975`, while `task-composite` — the SAME fixture file, one await instead of two — passes; the pre-cutover base `7df10725` runs both. Mechanism: `rt_task_clone` only bumps a refcount, so a handle and its clone poll the same `rt_task`; `rt_far_task_take_result` re-serves the same `producer->result_bits` on every take, because the field is never cleared and the local path carries no one-shot guard; and Wave C made RECEIVING a composite result an OWNERSHIP TRANSFER — `emitI64ToValue` now routes to `emitAdoptFromTransportAllocation`, which is memcpy-out plus `rt_free` of the carrier, where pre-cutover it was a bare `inttoptr` that transferred nothing. Two awaits, one carrier, two frees. The invariant being violated is broader than composites and should be stated that way: **a task result delivered N times must produce N independently owned values.** Verified not to be composite-specific — a cloned `Task<string>` awaited twice double-frees through `rt_string_free` by the same route, with no transport allocation involved at all. TWO CORRECTIONS to the obvious fix, both measured, so they are not rediscovered the hard way. (a) Replacing adopt with a non-freeing copy at the task-result take sites is correct ONLY for inert payloads: for a payload owning inner heap it MOVES the crash rather than removing it, since two awaiters then hold two shallow copies of the same inner pointers and each drops them. (b) The far-task take at `emit_crossing_far_task.go:160` must NOT be included: `rt_far_task_adopt_result` is one-shot, and applying the change there is a measured regression that leaks 4,032 bytes in 63 blocks on `far-task-composite`. The three sites that can repeat a delivery are the LOCAL takes at `emit_async.go:275` (Await), `:345` (Poll) and `:461` (Timeout). | Open | Epic 23b Wave C tail (task result ownership), BEFORE Wave C closes | A cloned task handle awaited from both handles yields two independently owned results, for an inert composite payload, for a payload owning inner heap (a struct with a `string` field), and for a bare `Task<string>` — each exiting 0 with Valgrind reporting zero bytes lost and zero invalid frees, and each printing the value twice rather than once-and-garbage. The carrier's ownership is stated somewhere a reader can find it: whoever holds the result is responsible for it until the last delivery, not the first awaiter to arrive. A row that awaits a cloned handle exactly once does not close this. |

| RV2-DEBT-168 | A LATENT COUPLING that is safe today only because a second component refuses to do something it will plausibly be asked to do. That should leak the duplicate, and it does not, because **sema never narrows a union's drop for a payload move-out**: `internal/sema/residual_drop.go:124` refuses to enumerate the parts of an array element or a union payload and falls back to the whole-value drop, documented there as deliberate — *"Refusing here is what keeps the caller on the whole-value drop rather than emitting a shallow free that abandons the live remainder."* So the union releases its payload, the binding's duplicate is released too, two owners and two drops, balanced. The hazard is that the balance depends on a refusal rather than on agreement: **the moment sema learns to enumerate union payload parts, the duplicate becomes a leak.** That enumeration is exactly what making partial moves out of unions a language shape would require, so the breaking change is one feature away and it is a feature somebody will plausibly want. | RV2-DEBT-166 | NONBLOCKING size regression with a SILENCED DETECTOR. The Wave C VM flip grew `vm.Value` from 72 to **104 bytes**, because `Location` now carries a `StorageRef` (arena pointer, offset, type, generation, alignment). `Value` is passed BY VALUE throughout the interpreter, so this is a real per-call copy cost on a hot path. `gocritic`'s `hugeParam` reported 143 instances across `internal/vm` and was **excluded for that package, for that check only** — every other gocritic check stays on there, and `hugeParam` stays on everywhere else. The exclusion is correct: the check's prescribed remedy is to pass a pointer, and a pointer to a `Value` is two names for one value, which is exactly the aliasing the inline representation exists to make impossible. The rejected alternative, 143 `//nolint` lines, would have been worse — each one invites a future reader to "fix" it by taking a pointer. But it silences the only signal that would report FURTHER growth: if `Value` reaches 200 bytes, nothing will say so, which is why this row exists rather than the decision simply being made in the lint config. The lane measured the reachable sizes rather than guessing: **96** by dropping the derivable `Align`, **88** by merging `Local`/`Global` and `Index`/`ByteOffset`, and **80** (the check's default threshold) only via redesigning `Location` into a real discriminated union. | Open | Epic 23b Wave D or later, VM storage representation | `Location` becomes a discriminated union and `Value` returns under the threshold, at which point the `internal/vm` exclusion is DELETED rather than kept; or the exclusion is ratified as permanent with a measured argument that the copy cost is acceptable on the interpreter's hot path. Either way the current size is recorded in the lint config so a later reader can distinguish growth from the baseline, and any future increase is a deliberate decision rather than an unobserved one. |
| RV2-DEBT-165 | BLOCKER REGRESSION introduced by the Wave C native cutover (`6cdb8b56`): **the cutover broke every suspension frame on the native backend — all of `async` and all of `blocking { }`, not one construct.** Confirmed by the lead in both directions: a thirteen-line pure-`async` program with no `blocking` anywhere prints `async ok` and exits 0 at the cutover's parent `35cee2cf`, and SEGFAULTS at `b54870c9`. Other measured symptoms on the same tree: `blocking` with a scalar capture and with no captures both segfault; `blocking` with a composite capture aborts with `double free or corruption (out)`; a pure-async variant reports `free(): invalid pointer`. ONE root cause with TWO faces, both being the same mistake — the suspension-frame exemption is honored where a type is SPELLED and ignored where one is MATERIALIZED. **(a) Class.** `blocking { }` additionally failed to COMPILE: `call contract for __blocking_block$N: parameter 0 classified byval but lowers as direct`. The backend knew frames are exempt from inline storage (`hasInlineStorage` consulting `mir.IsSuspensionFrameType`) while the shared MIR call contract's `classifyParam` decided on `IsValueComposite` alone. That predicate had exactly ONE consumer in the tree — the single backend line — so the exemption lived in a consumer and not in the contract its consumers must agree with. The emitter's refusal (`emit_call_layout.go:82-88`) fired correctly and by design; it is the only reason this surfaced instead of miscompiling. **(b) Storage, the larger half.** `emitStructLit` reserves storage through `emitStorageAlloca`, which gates on `IsValueComposite` and therefore gives a suspension frame a FUNCTION-SCOPED ALLOCA. The runtime then takes ownership of that pointer and frees it — `rt_async_blocking.c:305` stores rather than copies (`job->state = state`) and `:23-24` calls `rt_free` on it; `__task_create` owns its resume state box the same way (`internal/mir/lower_call_contracts.go:96`). Emitted IR shows both frames on the stack and handed to the runtime: `%t1 = alloca [24 x i8]` for the `__AsyncPayload$` union, `%t4 = alloca [32 x i8]` for the `__AsyncState$` struct, then `call ptr @__task_create(i64 5, ptr %t8)`. Pre-cutover a composite was heap-boxed, so a frame literal was a heap box BY ACCIDENT of the old representation and the runtime could legitimately own and free it; making composites inline moved frames onto the stack because the materialization sites never asked the frame question. **Why every gate was green, and this is the more important half:** nothing in the ordinary-storage corpus, the crossing gate, the goldens or carriergate RUNS a native async binary. That is consistent with the Wave C spike doc excluding task/channel/async-frame from the wave's scope — but it means the cutover was verified, including by the lead personally, by gates that structurally cannot see the surface it broke. The one test that catches the compile half, `TestMTBlockingPool` (`internal/vm/mt_correctness_test.go:457`), is a native e2e row `make check` hides under `SURGE_SKIP_TIMEOUT_TESTS=1`. Everything else mentioning `blocking` in `testdata/` is a NEGATIVE golden row that never reaches emission. The carrier benchmark found it because its fixtures are the only material in the tree that compiles a blocking block to LLVM. The VM is unexposed: nothing under `internal/vm` consumes `CallLayouts`/`OfSignature`/`SurgeABI`. | Open | Epic 23b Wave C tail (native backend), BEFORE Wave C integrates | One rule, honored at every site. A single predicate (`mir.LivesInInlineStorage` or equivalent) owned by the contract answers both the classification and the materialization question; `classifyParam`/`classifyResult` and `hasInlineStorage` both read it, and `emitStorageAlloca` REFUSES a type that does not live in inline storage rather than silently giving it a stack slot — so every site not yet found announces itself as a build error instead of as a silent stack allocation. Suspension frames get an explicit runtime-owned heap path. The emitter's byval/direct refusal stays untouched. Evidence is COMPILES AND RUNS, not compiles: pure `async`, `blocking` with a scalar capture, with no captures, and with a composite capture all exit 0 under Valgrind-clean teardown, plus `TestMTBlockingPool` green with `SURGE_SKIP_TIMEOUT_TESTS=0`. A native async row and a native blocking row are added where an ordinary run can see them, because `make check` structurally cannot. |

| RV2-DEBT-163 | A catch-all `compare` arm that binds a union payload reads from offset 0 instead of the payload offset, producing WRONG DATA silently. In `Erring<Option<string>, Error>` the binding `err` is typed `Error` (16 bytes) but MIR emits a whole-value `Move` from the 24-byte scrutinee with an empty projection list — no `TagPayload` projection — so the backend faithfully copies 16 bytes from offset 0, which is the tag and the start of the payload rather than the payload. The read is in bounds, so it is memory-safe; it is simply the wrong bytes. Either sema's type for the binding is wrong or the lowering is missing the projection. This is a MIR/sema defect, not a backend one, and it PREDATES the wave: under the boxed representation both sides of the move were `ptr`, so the same missing projection was an invisible pointer copy that happened to work. Wave C's inline storage is what made it observable — the fourth pre-existing defect this wave has exposed by removing the indirection that was hiding it. Found by the LLVM lane while triaging its cutover. | Open | Sema/MIR union-payload binding, sequenced after the Wave C integration | A catch-all arm binding a union payload reads the payload, not the tag: either the binding's type or the lowering's projection is corrected, whichever is wrong; a VM/native parity row covers a catch-all binding over a union whose payload is narrower than the scrutinee, and asserts the bound value's contents rather than only its size. |

### Epic 23b Owner Overrides (2026-08-04)

| ID | Debt | Closed By | Evidence |
