| RV2-DEBT-143 | A blocking task could lose completion between its first terminal-status check and waiter registration: the blocking worker published DONE/CANCELLED and drained an empty key, after which the poller registered and parked forever. This was pre-existing and blocks raw execution of the frozen carrier `EPIC_BASE`; fixing the product bug does not silently authorize changing or overlaying that benchmark base. | Runtime V2 blocker pass, commit `877e974c` | `poll_blocking_task` now registers then acquire-rechecks terminal state and unlinks its waiter before observing the terminal result. `TestRuntimeV2CarrierBenchBlockingRegisterThenVerify` deterministically passes the fixed order; `RV2_DEBT_143_NEGATIVE_CONTROL` restores the old order and must strand with the named failure. The exact benchmark-base disposition remains an explicit owner checkpoint. |
| RV2-DEBT-065 | SOUNDNESS + diagnostic hole, pre-existing, found 2026-07-23 by the adversarial review of RV2-DEBT-064 and CONFIRMED independently by direct e2e probe. A select (or compare) arm that is CLOSED — one that exits via `return` rather than producing a value — never contributes its moves to the union moved-set: the merge loop skips `armClosed[i]` entries (`internal/sema/type_expr_select.go` ~88-96; the same skip exists on the compare path, `type_expr_flow.go` ~99-108). A binding moved into a closed arm therefore stays LIVE past the join. Probe (local `Channel<string>`, `ch.send(own u) => { return 5; }` as the closed arm, recv arm wins): the program COMPILES with no diagnostic and `print(u)` after the select prints the moved payload's contents — where the identical select with an OPEN send arm correctly errors SEM3130 use-of-moved-value. Two consequences: the use-after-move diagnostic is silently absent for this shape, and the review reports the payload is additionally freed by the losing-arm reclamation while compiled code still holds the binding (double free + UAF); the diagnostic hole is confirmed here, the memory-safety half is the review's finding and was not independently reproduced in the probe run (which printed correct contents and exited 0). CLOSED 2026-07-23, with the severity CORRECTED DOWN and independently corroborated. `armClosed` plays two roles and only one was wrong. Skipping a closed arm in the MERGE is correct for ordinary flow-sensitivity — control never reaches the join through it, so its BODY-local moves must not poison the post-join state. But an arm's AWAIT is evaluated before the select runs, so a move there is unconditional: a `send(own x)` payload is handed to `rt_select_poll` (local) or shipped in the far arm table before any winner exists, exactly as `checkSelectSendPayloadOwnership`'s own diagnostic text promises ("if this arm loses, the value is reclaimed in the other arms and cannot be used after the select"). FIX: `typeSelectExpr` snapshots each arm's moved-set once more between typing the arm's await and typing its body (`movedAwait[i]`), and the merge unions that await-half back in for CLOSED arms while still ignoring their body-local moves; nothing merges when every arm is closed, since the join is then unreachable. The position IS the invariant, which is why no per-call-site bookkeeping is needed. LOAD-BEARING NEIGHBOUR, now commented in the code: the drop-synthesis loop must keep skipping closed arms — a closed arm's own abrupt exit already runs `recordEarlyExitDrops` against its moved-set, so also giving it an `ArmDrops` entry would drop the other arms' payloads twice. The merge deliberately reads closed arms (await half only) while the drop loop deliberately does not. MECHANISM CORRECTION (found by adversarial review, verified here): `break` does NOT close an arm at all — `compareArmAbruptExit` -> `blockAbruptStatus` -> `flowStatus` in abrupt-exit mode treats only `return`, `panic(...)`, `exit(...)` and an all-abrupt `compare` as closed, and `StmtBreak`/`StmtContinue` fall through to `returnOpen`. So a `break` arm is an OPEN arm, is merged, and `rejectLoopBackEdgeMoves` already saw its move — which is why the loop shape appeared "already defended" during the first investigation. The `return`-closed loop variant was NOT defended: a payload bound outside a loop and moved by a `return`-closed send arm inside it COMPILED before this fix and is correctly refused after (A/B verified with a pre-fix compiler). That back-edge bypass is a second, real diagnostic hole this fix closes. NEGATIVE CONTROL: new golden fixture `testdata/golden/sema/invalid/select_send_closed_arm_use_after_join` — SEM3130 with the fix, an EMPTY regenerated `.diag` with the fix stashed. SEVERITY: the originating review called this double free + UAF. NOT CONFIRMED, and now corroborated as leak-or-nothing by an independent pass that BUILT AND RAN thirteen shapes under valgrind (local and far; closed arm winning and losing; closed recv arm winning while an open send arm loses — the RV2-DEBT-064 disown case; two closed arms plus one open; a `default` arm winning; cancel-while-pending; the loop variant) and found no invalid free, no invalid read/write, and no differential leak anywhere: the reclaim merely relocated between an `ArmDrops` entry and a scope-exit or early-exit drop, still exactly once. The mechanism that makes it safe is that `armClosed` implies control leaves the FUNCTION, so a winning closed arm's `return` runs `recordEarlyExitDrops` against a moved-set where the payload IS moved. This is therefore a contract-fidelity and diagnostic-consistency fix, NOT a memory-safety fix, and must not be described as closing a soundness hole. REGRESSION EVIDENCE: `internal/sema`, `internal/hir`, `internal/mir` green; the far-select, drop-select, remote-select and strict-census runtime rows green; `runtime-v2-crossing-check` and `runtime-v2-transport-check` green; a pre/post diagnostic sweep over all 694 `.sg` fixtures under `testdata/golden/sema` and `testdata/golden/crossing` differs on exactly one file — the new fixture. RESIDUAL, recorded not fixed: an `async { }` block hosting the select, where a `return` closes the arm but exits only the block while the payload binding lives in the enclosing function, is the one construct where "closed" would not mean "left the binding's scope". It cannot be built today — async blocks cannot capture channels (`mir: unknown local symbol` on the channel) — so the interaction is untestable; if that capture gap is ever closed, re-test this. | Closed (await-half moves survive a closed arm; body-local moves still don't) | Epic 21 follow-up | A binding moved into a closed select send arm is use-of-moved after the join, with a diagnostic negative control and a reclamation row proving exactly one owner. |
| RV2-DEBT-066 | Per-arm drop synthesis and early-exit drop synthesis can double up on the SAME binding: an arm containing a conditional `return` gets a `wrapArmDrops` drop at the arm head (`internal/hir/lower_expr.go` ~46-62) and a `recordEarlyExitDrops` drop on the return path (`internal/sema/drop_obligations.go` ~224-252). Reported by the RV2-DEBT-064 adversarial review with a MIR reading (`bb2: drop L4; if ... bb8` / `bb8: drop L4`), described as not remote-select-specific and likely reaching compare arms too. NOT INDEPENDENTLY REPRODUCED: an e2e probe built for exactly this shape (local `Channel<string>` select, losing non-Copy send arm, recv arm containing `if c { return 5; }`) ran clean under valgrind — no invalid free, the payload reclaimed exactly once; the only definitely-lost blocks were the known local channel-object class. So either the MIR observation names two drops of different bindings, or the discriminating shape is narrower than the probe used. Needs a shape that actually fails before any fix. Matters because RV2-DEBT-064's fix makes compiled code the SOLE owner of losing arm payloads on the winner path, so a compiled-side double drop would no longer be masked by anything. RESOLVED 2026-07-23: the MIR reading was RIGHT and the conclusion drawn from it was WRONG — there is a genuine duplicate, and it is harmless by construction. Reproduced exactly: for `select { ch.send(own s) => 1; rx.recv() => { if c { return 5; } ret 2; }; }` the emitted `run$poll` has `bb2: drop L9; if copy L0 then bb8 else bb9` and `bb8: drop L9; goto bb26`, where `L9` is `s`, the losing send arm's payload — two drops of the same local on ONE taken path (the arm-head drop from `wrapArmDrops`, then the early-exit drop from `recordEarlyExitDrops`). It does not double free because `emitInstrDrop` (`internal/backend/llvm/emit_instr.go`) ends every drop with `store ptr null` into the dropped slot, and each drop entry point tolerates NULL (`rt_string_free` returns immediately on NULL, `rt_far_channel_handle_drop` likewise). The second drop therefore loads NULL and no-ops. Measured: the program runs valgrind-clean on the doubled path — no invalid free, no invalid read/write, the only definitely-lost blocks being the known local channel-object class. (An earlier e2e probe of this shape also ran clean; that was correct, not a missed reproduction.) CLOSED as not-a-defect, with one thing worth stating loudly because it is now load-bearing: drop idempotence in this compiler rests ENTIRELY on that null-after-drop store plus null tolerance in every drop entry point. Any future drop path that frees without nulling its slot, or any drop callee that stops checking NULL, turns every redundant drop of this kind into a real double free. The redundancy itself is a MIR quality wart (a dead instruction on that path), not a correctness bug, and is not worth fixing on its own. | Closed (real duplicate, harmless: drop nulls its slot and every drop callee tolerates NULL) | Resolved during Epic 21 follow-up triage | Either a failing shape is produced and the overlap fixed with a negative control, or the overlap is shown impossible and the review's MIR reading explained. |
| RV2-DEBT-067 | `oneSidedDroppables` (`internal/sema/drop_obligations.go` ~327-342) checks `isDroppableBinding` and `bindingDeclaredAtOrAbove` but NOT `isAliasedBinding`, so a binding that merely ALIASES container-owned state (its value came from a field/element/deref read — the case `markAliasedBinding` exists for) can receive a synthesized arm drop and free memory its container still owns. Reported by the RV2-DEBT-064 adversarial review from code reading; NOT reproduced with a failing program here. CLOSED 2026-07-23, and it was NOT speculative — REPRODUCED as real memory corruption. Shape: `type Holder = { name: string }; let h = Holder { name: build(...) }; let x: string = h.name; select { ch.send(own x) => 1; rx.recv() => 2; }` with the send arm losing, then `print(h.name)`. Under valgrind: multiple `Invalid read` plus an `Invalid free` — the arm-drop synthesized into the winning recv arm frees the string the struct still owns, the later field read touches freed memory, and the container's own drop frees it again. So this is use-after-free plus double free, not a latent code smell. FIX: reject the payload at the ownership check rather than at the drop synthesis. `checkSelectSendPayloadOwnership` (`internal/sema/type_expr_select.go`) now refuses an aliased binding with SEM3141, immediately after the existing whole-binding/projection check — an alias is that same hazard one binding removed. Rejecting at the source is what the fix needs to be, because skipping the drop (the review's suggested `isAliasedBinding` guard inside `oneSidedDroppables`) would only fix the LOSING arm: on the winning arm the payload is genuinely delivered to the channel while the container still owns it, so the double free happens anyway. NEGATIVE CONTROL: new golden fixture `testdata/golden/sema/invalid/select_send_aliased_payload`. REGRESSION: `internal/sema`/`hir`/`mir` green, the far-select, drop-select, remote-select and strict-census runtime rows green, `runtime-v2-crossing-check` and `runtime-v2-transport-check` green. RESIDUAL, deliberately not fixed: `oneSidedDroppables` (`internal/sema/drop_obligations.go`) still does not consult `isAliasedBinding`, unlike its sibling `liveDroppables` which skips aliases explicitly. With the source rejected, no select shape can reach it with an alias today, but the asymmetry remains and compare-arm drop synthesis shares that function — no reproducing shape was found there, so the guard was not added blind. | Closed (rejected at the ownership check, SEM3141; reproduced first) | Resolved during Epic 21 follow-up triage | An aliased binding cannot reach arm-drop synthesis, proven by a diagnostic row on `let x = s.field; ... send(own x)`, or the alias case is shown unreachable there. |
| RV2-DEBT-068 | A bignum payload delivered into a channel is never reclaimed: `typeOwnsHeap` returns false for a bignum `int`/`uint`/`float` (`internal/backend/llvm/emit_drop_glue.go` ~63-97 falls through to the `default:` case), so the `channel_on::<T>`/`make_channel::<T>` lowering computes drop-fn id 0 for such a T and RV2-DEBT-064-era buffer drain at `rt_channel_free` has nothing to dispatch. Measured 2026-07-23 while building `TestRuntimeV2FarSelectConstArmEvaluatedOnce`: a single `int` literal outside the fixnum inline range, sent through a far channel, leaves exactly `36 bytes in 1 block` definitely-lost, stack `rt_bigint_from_literal` <- `bi_alloc` (`rt_bignum_int.c`). That row pins the count at one deliberately — it gates the crossing block's const-operand evaluation, not this reclamation gap — so the gap is visible and bounded rather than hidden. Distinct from the resolved pervasive int/uint leak (small-int inline/fixnum work): this is specifically the drop-glue classification refusing to treat a heap bignum as heap-owning. SCOPE CORRECTED 2026-07-23, and it is much WIDER than the channel-payload framing above: the same 36 bytes leak from a PLAIN LOCAL with no channel, no crossing and no async involved — `@entrypoint fn main() -> int { let big: int = <literal beyond the fixnum range>; ... }` leaks 36 bytes in 1 block, stack `bi_alloc` (`rt_bignum_int.c`). So this is not a channel defect at all; heap-backed bignums are simply never dropped anywhere in the language. The reason is structural rather than an oversight: an `int` word is either an inline fixnum (odd tag) or a heap bignum pointer (even, non-NULL), so whether a value owns heap depends on the VALUE, not the type — which is exactly what `typeOwnsHeap` classifies. Making it true for `int`/`uint`/`float` turns EVERY integer in every program into a droppable, each drop needing a runtime fixnum-tag check before it may free. That is a language-wide ownership decision with real codegen and performance consequences, not a local fix, and it should be scoped and reviewed as its own vertical rather than folded into a channel row. Relatives: the resolved pervasive-leak work (fixnum inlining) deliberately made the common case allocation-free instead of solving drops, and RV2-DEBT-035 (refcounting) is the other candidate mechanism. DIRECTION CHOSEN 2026-07-23 by the project owner, after a survey of how other languages solve this: COPY-ON-WRITE VALUE TYPES, i.e. Swift's model — the heap block carries a reference count, `let b = a` keeps both usable with no move errors and no surface-language change, a borrowing read never clones (the arithmetic entry points already take `const void*`), drop obligations extend to these scalars, and the block frees at zero. This is the same direction RV2-DEBT-038 independently sketched for `float`. Two framing facts that decided it. FIRST, the survey: every language offering a seamless dynamic int has a GARBAGE COLLECTOR — Ruby, OCaml, Lisp and Smalltalk use the identical tagged fixnum/bignum representation Surge uses and simply GC the heap half; the languages without a GC all drop one leg instead, either making big numbers an explicit non-Copy type (Rust's `BigInt`, C++ with GMP+RAII) or fixing the width (Go, C, Java, Swift's trapping `Int`). The combination Surge wants — seamless dynamic int, value semantics, no GC — is implemented by nobody, so this is a choice of which leg to release, not a search for a trick. Swift's copy-on-write containers are the proven precedent for value semantics over a heap payload without a GC. SECOND, and this is what makes it viable here where it would not be elsewhere: the reference count can plausibly be NON-ATOMIC, which removes the apparent conflict with RUNTIME_V2.md's rule that request-path code must not touch shared refcounts. The argument is that a bignum reaches another shard only through an EXPLICIT crossing (`on`, `spawn on`, far channels), so every crossing can deep-copy and no block is ever shared across shards. That claim is LOAD-BEARING AND NOT YET VERIFIED — it is the first thing the pending adversarial review must break or confirm, since a single path that publishes a pointer without cloning forces an atomic counter and reopens the conflict. SEQUENCING NOTE: `float` is the worse and more urgent half (unbounded, ~2 blocks per operation, no inline form possible) while `int` leaks only retained large values, so float may be the smaller vertical that proves the mechanism. The alternative the owner rejected was ratifying the leak; rejected on the stated rule that a pre-existing defect surviving the refactor is worse than an incomplete V2 feature, and that engineering expense alone is not grounds to defer a real bug. | Open | Drop-glue type classification | `typeOwnsHeap` recognises heap bignums, a bignum channel payload reclaims exactly once, and the const-arm row's residual tightens from 36 bytes to zero. |
| RV2-DEBT-069 | Remote-select commit recording has a window the shutdown/cancel sweep can steal: `record_select_commit` (`runtime/native/rt_far_channel_select.c` ~290-308) only writes `select_committed_index` when the pending is still `PENDING`, but `rt_select_poll`'s commit has already released its own critical section by then. If `rt_remote_task_fail_all_pending` (`rt_remote_task_wait.c`) resolves the pending inside that window, the index stays `NO_COMMIT`, RV2-DEBT-064's winner gate correctly does NOT fire (the status is no longer OK), and the release loop then drops the payload of the arm the owner ALREADY delivered into the channel — a double free reachable only through shutdown racing a committing select. Found by the RV2-DEBT-064 adversarial review; pre-existing relative to that fix and not introduced by it. REPRODUCED DETERMINISTICALLY 2026-07-23, and the obvious fix is REFUTED. Recipe (rebuildable in minutes; the row itself was not kept, since it would be permanently red): in the publication harness, give `select_exec_state` a `send_drop_fn_ids[2]` array and thread it into `rt_far_channel_select`; make `__surge_drop_result_call` count instead of `exit(97)` so arm drops are assertable; set up a one-arm SEND select on a capacity-1 far channel so the send commits immediately; arm `SP_FAR_SELECT_AFTER_COMMIT_BEFORE_REPLY:block`; from the driver, wait for the window, take an extra pending reference, call `rt_remote_task_fail_all_pending(ex, DESTINATION_SHUTDOWN)` DIRECTLY (going through `rt_executor_request_shutdown` deadlocks — shutdown waits for workers to quiesce and the worker you need is the parked one), then open the point and await. Result: `arm_drops=1`, `select_committed_index=UINT64_MAX` (NO_COMMIT) — the committed arm's payload is dropped although the channel already owns it, exactly as this row predicted. WHY THE NAIVE FIX FAILS: removing the `status == PENDING` condition from `record_select_commit`'s match does NOT help. Instrumentation shows the function is entered (`RSC: entered`) but its candidate loop prints nothing — `state->pending_head` is already EMPTY, so the pending has been unlinked from the registry before the body resumes; a driver-held reference keeps the memory alive but not the listing. A registry scan therefore cannot find it at that point, whatever the status test says. FIX DIRECTION (identified, not implemented): the body must not re-scan. `rt_anchored_channel_select` already calls `rt_remote_task_select_binding_current` at body entry, BEFORE any sweep can land; extend that lookup to hand back the pending with an added reference, hold it across `rt_select_poll`, record the commit through the pointer directly, and release. Note the same registry scan with the same `status == PENDING` condition appears TWICE in that file (the binding lookup and the commit record), which is what makes a textual patch to one of them silently hit the wrong site. Second, smaller item from the same review, recorded here rather than as its own row: `rt_remote_task_pending_set_reply` (`rt_remote_task_pending.c` ~187-201) is not status-gated and writes `result_kind`/`result_bits` unconditionally — "exactly one reply edge" is documented but never asserted, so a second reply edge would flip the kind after the winner gate's peek. An assert or status guard there is cheap. | Open | Remote-select commit/teardown race | The commit index survives a teardown that lands in the window (or the window is closed), proven with a forced sync point; the reply edge asserts its single-write contract. |
| RV2-DEBT-070 | `make golden-check` is NONDETERMINISTIC and therefore an unreliable gate: it runs `golden-update` and then diffs against git, and the SAME tree regenerates different content run to run. Measured 2026-07-23: three consecutive runs on one unchanged tree gave exit 0, 2, 0; the differing file was `testdata/golden/mir/compare_guard_await_release.mir` and the diff was pure identifier renumbering — `type#1514` vs `type#1510` and a rotation of drop-function ids (`const 4`/`5`/`6` permuted), never a change in which instructions or drops exist. The fixture contains no `select` and was unrelated to the change being tested, so the numbering is shared across fixtures rather than per-file, and the assignment order varies between runs. Consequence: a genuine golden regression is indistinguishable from a flake on a single run, and a real change to MIR output can be masked by a run that happens to match. Discovered while validating RV2-DEBT-065, where it first presented as a false regression. Distinct from the closed RV2-DEBT-045 (module-record dedupe for stdlib diagnostics); this is MIR type/drop-fn id assignment order. ROOT CAUSE LOCALISED 2026-07-23, and it is WORSE THAN A GATE BUG: the COMPILER ITSELF is nondeterministic. `scripts/golden_update.sh` is sequential and compiles each fixture in its OWN `surge` process, so nothing is shared across fixtures — the flake is inside a single invocation. Measured directly: `surge diag --format short --emit-mir testdata/golden/mir/compare_guard_await_release.sg` on an unchanged tree produced DIFFERING output in 7 of 20 runs, settling into exactly two stable variants; the differing block is the async state types (`__state_init`/`__payload_init`), shifted by exactly 4 TypeIDs, plus a permutation of drop-function ids. Stage bisection over the same file: `--emit-instantiations` 1 distinct output of 8, `--emit-hir` 1 of 8, `--emit-mono` 6 of 8. The mono report's own diff is pure LINE ORDER — the TypeID multiset is identical — so mono's printing iterates a map, while the id divergence itself arrives later. MECHANISM: `Module.Funcs` is a `map[FuncID]*Func` (`internal/mir/module.go`), Go randomises map iteration, and many passes range over it WHILE ALLOCATING identifiers — async lowering builds state types (`internal/mir/async_lowering_single.go`), and the LLVM backend assigns drop-function ids (`internal/backend/llvm/emit_collect.go`, `emit_globals.go`). Whichever function is lowered first gets the lower ids. PARTIAL FIX PROBED AND REVERTED: sorting the async-lowering iteration by `FuncID` reduced but did NOT eliminate the divergence (still 2 distinct outputs of 12), confirming multiple allocating iteration sites; a half-fix was not kept because it would mask the remainder without closing it. CONSEQUENCE beyond the gate: builds are not reproducible, so any artifact comparison — golden, MIR/IR diffing, bisecting a codegen regression — is unreliable, and a real regression is indistinguishable from a flake on a single run. MIR HALF FIXED 2026-07-23 (commit `7c75aa99`): `Module.SortedFuncIDs`, `MonoModule.SortedFuncKeys` and `MonoModule.SortedTypeKeys` were added and all 28 walks routed through them, with a source-shape gate (`TestCompilerWalksFuncsInDeterministicOrder`, `internal/mir`) keeping the direct form from returning — it exempts the `Sorted*` helpers themselves and `hir.Module`, whose `Funcs` is a slice, and its negative control is reintroducing one direct walk. Measured: the known-flaky fixture went from 3 distinct outputs in 20 runs to 20 identical; a sweep of all 884 golden fixtures under `--emit-mir` reports 0 nondeterministic at N=3 where it previously flagged one; `make golden-check` passes on repeated runs where it previously alternated exit 0 and 2 on an unchanged tree. Committed goldens needed NO regeneration — the sorted order matches what was already checked in. ROW STAYS OPEN for a narrower remainder: `--emit-mono` is still nondeterministic (7 distinct of 12 on the same fixture). That report's own collections are ordered now and its comparators sort by `OrigSym`, so the divergence has moved into those sort KEYS — symbol ids assigned during monomorphization are themselves order-dependent, a deeper root than the walks fixed here. Its type listing additionally carries DUPLICATE entries (8 rows for 4 distinct types, the duplicate groups swapping position), which is worth understanding on its own and may be the same root. No golden fixture compares `--emit-mono` today, so the remainder is a debug-dump defect rather than a gate one; the reproducible-build claim now holds for MIR and the LLVM backend. Note the reviewer's second sighting (a `make golden` that exited 0 having printed only ">> Building surge") is most likely their own killed run, not this defect — recorded so it is not double-counted as evidence. REMAINDER ROOT-CAUSED AND CLOSED 2026-07-30. The remainder was NOT what this row predicted: measurement refutes the "symbol ids assigned during monomorphization are themselves order-dependent" hypothesis. All 20 runs of `--emit-mono` on the flaky fixture are content-identical MODULO LINE ORDER — the printed `sym=` (InstanceSym) and `type#` values are stable, only the order of whole GROUPS moved, with order INSIDE each group fixed. That shape says the SORT KEY moved, not the ids being sorted: the mono dump orders by `OrigSym`, and `OrigSym` is a symbol id from the resolver, not one minted by monomorphization. Probing the symbol arena directly (id -> name for all 2849 symbols, repeated runs) showed the name multiset identical and a BLOCK of ~700 ids permuted, starting at id 721 — core's exports. MECHANISM: `injectCoreExports` (`internal/symbols/resolve_module_members.go`) walked `fr.moduleExports` and `exports.Symbols` — BOTH maps — calling `syntheticSymbolForExport`, which allocates from a bump arena, so every core symbol's id depended on Go's randomised map order; the same defect sat in `declareImportAll` (`resolve_imports.go`) for `import *`. Note `exportsPrelude`, ten lines below the offender in the same file, ALREADY sorted both collections — the fix is that walk's shape, now shared as `ModuleExports.SortedNames` and `sortedCoreModulePaths`. This is the same CLASS as the 2026-07-23 half fix but a different LAYER: that one ordered walks over `Module.Funcs` in mir/mono/backend, this one orders symbol declaration in the resolver, which sits upstream of everything. GATE: `TestSameSourceGetsSameSymbolIDs` (`internal/driver`) compiles one source TWICE IN ONE PROCESS and compares symbol ids — Go re-randomises each execution of a `range`, so one process suffices. It is a BEHAVIOURAL gate, unlike the source-shape `TestCompilerWalksFuncsInDeterministicOrder`, which is why it catches this class wherever it appears rather than at one known syntax; its negative control is reverting either sort, which fails it at exactly id 721. Measured after the fix: `--emit-mono` on the flaky fixture 30/30 identical (was 11 distinct of 20); a sweep of all 888 golden fixtures at N=3 reports 0 nondeterministic under BOTH `--emit-mono` and `--emit-mir`; the symbol arena is byte-identical across runs. Committed goldens needed NO regeneration — the sorted order again matches what was already checked in. The duplicate type rows in the mono dump (8 rows for 4 types) are now STABLE but still duplicated, and are a distinct defect tracked as RV2-DEBT-092. | Closed | Golden gate determinism | The same tree regenerates byte-identical goldens across repeated runs, proven by a repeat-run check, or the id numbering is made per-fixture so cross-fixture order cannot leak. |
| RV2-DEBT-071 | WITHDRAWN 2026-07-23, SAME DAY IT WAS FILED — the measurement was right and my attribution was WRONG. The row claimed that arithmetic on inline integers round-trips through the heap via `bi_promote`. It does not: `rt_bigint_add` and `rt_bigint_cmp` (`runtime/native/rt_bignum_int_api.c`) both open with an allocation-free fast path — `fixi_as_i64(a) && fixi_as_i64(b) && !__builtin_add_overflow(...)` returning `bi_from_i64_tagged(r)` — so `bi_promote` is never reached for in-range operands. Verified by direct read after an adversarial review challenged the attribution. THE REAL CAUSE IS LITERAL RE-PARSING, which is already RV2-DEBT-036. Every literal is materialized at runtime by `rt_bigint_from_literal`, which parses the DECIMAL STRING on each evaluation; `parse_uint_string` (`rt_bignum_parse.c` ~76-101) loops per digit doing `bu_mul_small` then `bu_add_small`, each allocating a fresh `SurgeBigUint` and freeing the previous — two allocations per decimal digit, verified by reading the loop. The original measurement decomposes exactly: `while i < 100000 { i = i + 1; }` re-parses a 6-digit literal in the condition (12) and a 1-digit literal in the body (2) = 14 per iteration. CONTROL, measured: hoisting both literals into bindings (`let n = 100000; let one = 1;`) takes the same 100,000-iteration program from `1,400,015` allocations to `17` for the entire run — zero per iteration. So there is no arithmetic defect here; the evidence belongs to RV2-DEBT-036 and is recorded there. Kept as a withdrawn row rather than deleted, because the misattribution had a consequence worth not repeating: it was used, briefly, as an argument that copy-on-write's cost would hide behind existing allocation traffic. That argument is void — after the literal fix an inline loop allocates nothing, so nothing hides. The correct argument for the same conclusion is that retain/release on an inline fixnum is one predictable not-taken branch and never touches the counter at all. | Withdrawn (misattributed; evidence moved to RV2-DEBT-036) | — | — |
| RV2-DEBT-072 | A boxed composite NESTED inside another never frees its inner box. `emitFieldDropAt` (`internal/backend/llvm/emit_drop_glue.go`) skips a field whose `typeOwnsHeap` is false, which is exactly the shape of a struct/tuple whose own fields hold no heap — so `type Nested = { inner: Pair, label: int }` leaks one 16-byte block per construction even though the OUTER box is now reclaimed. Discovered 2026-07-24 while closing the top-level case; the outer half is fixed and gated by `TestRuntimeV2CompositeBoxIsReclaimed`, which pins struct and tuple at strict zero and deliberately omits the nested shape. Closing this means making `typeOwnsHeap` true for boxed composites generally, which switches drop glue on for nearly every type at once and must be sequenced AFTER RV2-DEBT-073 (the Copy aliasing question), since a `@copy` composite would otherwise be dropped through two aliases. CLOSED 2026-07-27 by Epic 23 step 6. The fix is not in the field walk at all: `typeOwnsHeap` now answers true for a VALUE COMPOSITE before it reaches that walk, so a nested composite is reclaimed because it owns its box, not because a field of it happens to own heap. The narrower structural question the walk asks — "does a field own heap" — was the wrong one for this shape and stays only for deciding which FIELDS a drop must free. Measured: 16 nested constructions in a loop report `All heap blocks were freed -- no leaks are possible`. | Open | Drop glue field walk | Closed (a value composite owns its box) | A nested composite's inner box is freed; a nested-shape row in `TestRuntimeV2CompositeBoxIsReclaimed` passes at strict zero. |
| RV2-DEBT-073 | A `@copy` composite leaks its box, once per construction, and CANNOT simply be made droppable. Measured 2026-07-24: `@copy type Q = { a: int, b: int }` loses 256 bytes in 16 blocks over 16 constructions; with `float` fields it also loses the fields (1,920 bytes indirect). Reproduced unchanged at `9b849310`, i.e. this predates Epic 22 entirely and is not a refcounting artifact. The obstruction is representation, not classification: a boxed composite is duplicated by copying the box POINTER, so `let q = p` gives two bindings one box, and marking the type droppable would double-free. Sema's `ownsHeap` reports false for it (Copy wins), so no drop is even emitted today. Two ways out, both real decisions: unbox small Copy composites into registers so a copy is a value copy, or clone the box on every Copy-composite copy (the Swift path, and the same shape the arbitrary-precision scalars solve by counting). Blocks RV2-DEBT-072. CLOSED 2026-07-27 by Epic 23 Phase 1 (steps 1-6), which took the second way out the row names — clone the box on every Copy-composite copy — behind a permanent boundary rather than as a point fix. `OperandCopyValue` carries the consuming-copy intent that `OperandCopy` could not; both backends implement it (a fresh object on the VM, generated `@clone.typeN` glue on the native side, alloc + memcpy + fix up only the members whose bits are not the value); `ownsHeap` then flipped so the box is reclaimed. Neither half works alone, which is why they landed together: droppable-while-aliasing double-frees, independent-while-undroppable leaks. Measured: build/copy/drop and nested shapes report strict zero definitely-lost under valgrind, and copy independence holds on both backends from one source. The blocked row RV2-DEBT-072 closed with it. Phase 2 replaces the boxed clone with inline storage behind the same boundary; the contract tests do not change. | Open | Copy composite representation | Closed (type-directed copy + owned box) | A `@copy` struct is reclaimed exactly once per value with no double free; a `@copy` row in `TestRuntimeV2CompositeBoxIsReclaimed` passes at strict zero. |
| RV2-DEBT-074 | `compare` on a DEREFERENCED BORROW frees the referent, so the real owner's later drop is a double free — on the native backend, a use-after-free and usually a SIGSEGV. `compareScrutineeReleaseSafe` (`internal/hir/normalize_compare_release.go`) already refuses to release a borrowed scrutinee, but it tested only the scrutinee's TYPE: `*r` on an `&FmtArg` has type `FmtArg`, because the deref already stripped the reference, so the guard saw a bare union and concluded the compare owned the box. FILED 2026-07-27 WITH THE WRONG ATTRIBUTION and corrected the same day; the original row blamed "writing through a `&mut` composite parameter, then reading through it, in a function that returns a value", with four negative controls that appeared to isolate that conjunction. THE NEGATIVE CONTROLS WERE NOT CLEAN — they were silently corrupt: valgrind shows the identical Invalid read + Invalid free in the "passing" controls, which merely failed to segfault because the freed block happened to read back as a benign discriminant. Method note worth keeping: a crash/no-crash bisection over a heap-corruption bug attributes to whatever perturbs the allocator, not to the cause; the controls must be run under valgrind, not judged by exit code. The real blast radius is far larger than the original row implied — `core/format.sg`'s `append_fmt_arg` takes `arg: &FmtArg` and does `compare *arg`, so EVERY formatted print with a non-string argument was a double free, down to a five-line `let y = 100; print(f"{y}")` with no composites anywhere. CLOSED 2026-07-27. Fix: the guard now also inspects the scrutinee EXPRESSION (`scrutineeReadsThroughBorrow`), refusing release for a deref (and through an `own` wrapper), matching the file's stated errs-toward-false policy. Measured: the minimal int repro and the `&mut`-composite repro both go from SIGSEGV to correct output matching the VM, valgrind Invalid-read/Invalid-free counts drop to zero, and showcase `21_bigint_stress` goes from SIGSEGV to byte-identical with the VM. The 19-20 byte `rt_string_from_bigint` leak visible in these programs is PRE-EXISTING and unchanged (measured identical before and after). Gate: a `borrowed` probe in `TestRuntimeV2CompareScrutineeReleaseCensusBalanced` compares through `&Outcome` at n=1 and n=2000 and pins the alloc/free differential; negative control confirmed (reverting the guard makes it fail with a crash). Full `./internal/...` failure set is unchanged from baseline (33 vs 32 rows, the single difference being `TestMTStructuredConcurrency`, which flaked in the baseline run and passes 5/5 on re-run). WHY THE VM NEVER SAW IT: the VM's heap is reference-counted, so the extra release only decremented a count — which is exactly why the VM/LLVM differential could not catch this class and a census had to. | Open | Compare lowering (HIR normalize) | Closed (guard tests the scrutinee expression, not only its type) | A compare through a borrowed scrutinee frees nothing; the census probe balances at n=1 and n=2000; valgrind reports no invalid read or free for formatted printing. |
| RV2-DEBT-075 | A compare arm binding a REFCOUNTED-SCALAR payload out of a BORROWED union releases one reference too many, corrupting the heap. Split off from RV2-DEBT-074 on 2026-07-27 after that fix unblocked the path far enough to expose it. Repro is four lines: `let y: float = 1.5; print(f"{y}");` — no crash, correct output, and valgrind reports an Invalid read; showcase `23_roundtrip_parse_format` takes the same path and dies with `malloc(): unaligned tcache chunk detected` after printing nine correct lines. MECHANISM, read off the MIR of `append_fmt_arg`'s `FmtFloat` arm: `L23 = tag_payload copy L3.FmtFloat[0]` extracts a BARE ALIAS (no retain — `lowerTagPayloadExpr` in `internal/mir/lower_expr_misc.go` is the one extraction site that does NOT call `retainExtractedValue`, unlike `lowerFieldAccessExpr` and `lowerIndexExpr`), then `L22 = retain L23` gives the binding its own reference (+1), then `drop L23` releases the temp (-1), then `drop L22` releases the binding (-1) — net -1 against a union whose real owner still holds it. THE OBVIOUS FIX IS WRONG AND WAS TRIED AND REVERTED: adding `retainExtractedValue` to `lowerTagPayloadExpr` balances the borrowed case and breaks the OWNED case, where `drop L23` is not a bug but the ownership TRANSFER the envelope-release model depends on (the payload's reference moves to the binding and the envelope release deliberately does not touch it). With the unconditional retain, `TestRuntimeV2FloatReclamationValgrindZero` regresses from strict zero to 72 bytes in 3 blocks. So the retain must be CONDITIONAL on whether the scrutinee transfers ownership — the same predicate `compareScrutineeReleaseSafe` computes in HIR — and MIR's `lowerTagPayloadExpr` cannot see that today: both cases lower to an indistinguishable `tag_payload copy L3`. That is the design question this row carries, and it is the same shape as Epic 23's central finding (an extraction that aliases before the ownership decision is made), so the two should be solved with one mechanism rather than two. CLOSED 2026-07-27. Fix: the ownership answer is CARRIED DOWN from the compare instead of guessed at the extraction. `TagPayloadData` gains `SubjectBorrowed`, set from `!releaseSafe` where the tag arm is built and propagated through `lowerTagPayloadPattern` so a nested pattern inherits it (a payload read out of a borrowed union is itself borrowed, however deep it nests); `lowerTagPayloadExpr` retains only when that flag is set. Every site that copies `TagPayloadData` (mono clone/subst/traverse/dce, hir normalize, buildpipeline, driver) mutates a struct copy and reassigns, so the new field rides along with no change. Measured: `print(f"{1.5}")` valgrind-clean (Invalid count 0); showcase `23_roundtrip_parse_format` goes from `malloc(): unaligned tcache chunk detected` to byte-identical with the VM; all 30 showcases now build, run and exit clean on the native backend, versus 2 crashing before RV2-DEBT-074. Gate: two probes in `TestRuntimeV2CompareScrutineeReleaseCensusBalanced` that must BOTH stay balanced, `borrowed-float` (compare through `&Measure`) and `owned-float` (compare on an owned `Reading(...)`), at n=1 and n=2000. NEGATIVE CONTROLS BOTH WAYS, since a one-directional control would have passed the over-broad fix: removing the retain entirely crashes the borrowed probe; making it unconditional fails the owned-float probe AND regresses `TestRuntimeV2FloatReclamationValgrindZero` to 72 bytes in 3 blocks. Full `./internal/...` failure set is baseline minus one known flake. | Open | Compare arm payload extraction (HIR/MIR boundary) | Closed (the compare's ownership answer is carried to the extraction) | A float payload bound out of a borrowed union balances its references: `print(f"{1.5}")` is valgrind-clean, showcase `23_roundtrip_parse_format` matches the VM, and `TestRuntimeV2FloatReclamationValgrindZero` stays at strict zero. |
| RV2-DEBT-076 | Every crossing route REFUSES a `@copy` value composite instead of carrying it, as the deliberate safe state Epic 23 step 3 installed on 2026-07-27. A struct, tuple, union or fixed array is a value by the language's own definition but is still stored in a heap box, so shipping its word would hand two shards the same box — cross-shard aliasing today, and a cross-shard double free the moment step 6 makes the box droppable. Rather than ship that, `TriviallyTransportableBits` now excludes the combination (`Result.IsCopyValueComposite`: composite AND duplicable — a move-only composite still ships, because it transfers the box to exactly one shard, and a Copy scalar still ships, because its word is the value), and each route reports the representation as the cause rather than the misleading "not plain-copy data": `on`/`spawn on` and `blocking` captures at sema (SEM3168), and the crossing result, far-task-await result and remote channel element at the transport guard (FUT7020), with remote-select SEND arms inheriting the channel's element decision. THIS IS A DELIBERATE SOURCE BREAK: programs that compile today stop compiling. That is sanctioned for the 0.1.X -> 0.2.X refactor and is the honest trade — those programs are already silently sharing one box between two shards. The fix is Epic 23 step 7: install a deep copy at each boundary, which the type-directed clone of steps 4-5 makes available; Phase 2's inline representation then removes the need for the boundary clone entirely, since a composite crosses as inline bits plus its refcounted-scalar fields. Gated by three negative fixtures (`spawn_on_negative_copy_composite_capture`, `_spawn_on_negative_copy_composite_result`, `_channel_negative_copy_composite_element`) and one POSITIVE neighbour (`channel_positive_move_only_composite_element`) proving the refusal is narrow; negative control confirms all three go red when the predicate is disabled. NOTE a regression found and fixed inside this step: the first cut also refused `Placement`, which is declared as a struct but is `@intrinsic` and represented at runtime as a tagged word with no box, and whose entire purpose is to cross. Fixed in `Interner.IsValueComposite` rather than by special-casing the crossing sites, because the predicate was wrong about the type, not the sites. CLOSED 2026-07-28 by Epic 23 step 7. Every route carries a `@copy` value composite again, and the routes did NOT need the same treatment — reading the transport showed three shapes where the row assumed one. A CAPTURE is duplicated at its operand (`lower_expr_crossing.go`, now a consuming read for every capture mode, which also settles a disagreement: blocking captures always read consumingly and crossings did not), so the destination's state holds a box of its own. A RESULT is a TRANSFER — the body builds the box, the caller receives it, there is never a second holder — so it needed permission and no copy at all; adding one would have allocated a box per reply. A channel ELEMENT is duplicated at the send. The transport axis stopped excluding composites, and the sema capture refusals were removed. NOTE what step 7 had to add beyond lifting refusals: an unpacked COPY capture is now reclaimed at the crossing body's returns (`rewriteSpawnOnPollReturns`). The shallow envelope release rests on the stated assumption that "the captures were unpacked into locals, so only the box is dead", which was true while a capture was an alias and stopped being true once the state held a copy. That drop is restricted to COPY captures on purpose: an OWNED capture may be consumed by the body, move tracking is sema's and this synthetic local was never sema's to track, so dropping it unconditionally double-freed what the body handed on — measured as `free(): double free detected in tcache 2` across the migration suite before the restriction. Measured after: composite captured across `spawn on` is independent of the sender (body sees the pre-mutation value, sender keeps its own), strict-zero definitely-lost under valgrind, no memcheck error. Negative controls both directions: reverting the capture to a borrowing read fails independence, removing the capture drop leaks 32 bytes in 2 blocks. Full `./internal/...` diffed against pristine HEAD adds exactly one failure for the whole epic, the ownership-axis invariant test, updated to pin the new shape. STILL REFUSED, and out of this epic's scope: a composite carrying an arbitrary-precision scalar, because the crossing copy RETAINS such a field rather than deep-copying it and the count is not atomic — that refusal is Epic 22's. | Open | Crossing transport | Closed (each route gives the far side an owner) | Each route carries a `@copy` value composite again, by deep-copying at the boundary (step 7) or by inline representation (Phase 2); the three negative fixtures become positives and the positive neighbour still passes. |
| RV2-DEBT-077 | Reading a MOVE-ONLY composite out of a container yields an ALIAS, not a value, and the container stays live beside it. Repro: `type Inner = { x: int }; type Outer = { inner: Inner, label: int };` then `let o = Outer {...}; let mut e = o.inner; e.x = 99;` prints `o=99 e=99` — writing through the extracted binding is visible through the container. Identical on BOTH backends, so it is not a differential; measured 2026-07-27 while landing Epic 23 step 4, and PRE-EXISTING (the step touched only the Copy path, and the repro behaves the same with that change reverted). Distinct from the Copy case, which step 4 fixed: a Copy composite extraction now lowers to `OperandCopyValue` and duplicates, while this path lowers to `OperandMove` of a temp that already aliased the container, so no operand kind is wrong — the question is a LANGUAGE one the epic does not own. Moving a field out of a live struct is a partial move, and sema does not track partially-moved bindings, so it neither rejects the program nor invalidates `o`. Two defensible answers, and picking one is the work: reject the partial move until it is tracked (Rust's model marks the source partially moved and forbids whole-value use afterwards), or accept it and make the extraction a real transfer that invalidates the source field. This is why contract row 9 (extraction independence) is currently pinned for the Copy case only. Epic 23's inline representation does NOT close it by itself: an inline extraction copies the bits, which changes the symptom from a shared box to a silent bitwise duplicate of a move-only value — arguably worse, since it manufactures a second owner of any heap the fields hold. Decide the semantics before Phase 2 makes the copy free. CLOSED 2026-07-29 by Epic 24 step 8. The answer is the second of the two the row names: `own o.field` TRANSFERS, and the source place is invalidated. The extraction stops counting a second holder (`FieldAccessData.MoveOut`, sema -> HIR -> MIR, printed `field_move`), the receiving binding is no longer marked an alias, and the container drops only the places it still holds. Proven by `TestRuntimeV2PartialMoveReclaimsOnlyWhatItStillHolds` (valgrind, native, strict zero over six shapes). | Closed (a field read that takes ownership is a real transfer) |  A move-only composite field read out of a container either is rejected as an untracked partial move, or transfers and invalidates the source; contract row 9 covers the move-only shape as well as the Copy one. |
| RV2-DEBT-078 | A `compare` over a value-composite scrutinee LEAKS its duplicate: measured 2026-07-28 while freezing Epic 23's contract, 3 boxes per evaluation for `compare *h { Hold(p) => p; ... }` where `h: &Held` and `Held` is a `@copy` union of a `@copy` struct. Attributed by differential: a WILDCARD arm still loses 16 direct + 16 indirect blocks over 16 evaluations — that is the compare's own copy of the union, envelope plus the payload it owns — and a BINDING arm adds 16 more direct blocks, the binding's separate copy of the payload. A union with a scalar payload loses the envelope alone. So the two leaks are independent: the scrutinee copy leaks always, the payload-binding copy leaks only when the payload is itself a composite. Found because the freeze audited what each contract row is actually PINNED by, and rows 3, 4, 5 and 7 claimed reclamation properties that no census measured — the contract test runs without valgrind. Rows 3, 5 and 7 were leaking too and are fixed (see below); this is the shape that is not. MECHANISM, read off the MIR: the compare's synthetic `let __cmpN = v` now consumes a value composite, so `__cmpN` holds an independent CLONE rather than the alias it held when a composite read was a pointer copy. Two premises of `compareScrutineeReleaseSafe` are therefore stale for this shape — "the scrutinee reads through a borrow so we own nothing" and "the scrutinee is Copy so the original stays usable" — and both refuse the release that the clone now needs. Worse, the arm's payload binding is ALSO a clone (`L5 = copy_value L6`), while `tagPayloadReleaseShape` still classifies an all-bound arm as safe for a SHALLOW envelope release on the premise that the payload moved out to the binding; it did not, so the envelope's own payload box is abandoned as well. FIX ATTEMPTED AND REVERTED IN THE SAME SESSION: making the release predicate answer true for any value composite fixed part of the leak and broke two gates at once — `TestRuntimeV2CompareScrutineeReleaseCensusBalanced` crashed and the union-payload independence row failed — because it releases in cases the compare still does not own. The correct fix has to move shallow-vs-deep with the binding's behavior (a cloning binding leaves the payload in the envelope, so the release must be DEEP), which is the same delicate area as RV2-DEBT-052 and RV2-DEBT-075 and wants its own change rather than a tail-end patch. Not a correctness hazard beyond the leak: no invalid read or free at any point, on either backend. CLOSED 2026-07-30. Re-measured first, and this row's own numbers had gone stale: the BINDING-arm half was already fixed by RV2-DEBT-087, which landed after the row was filed. What remained was ONE leak, not two — the compare's own clone of the scrutinee, leaking on EVERY arm shape (over 16 evaluations: 16 direct blocks for the envelope, plus 16 indirect when the payload is itself a composite; a scalar payload loses the envelope alone). The `@copy` attribute is what selects the shape: a plain move-only union read through the same deref does not leak. FIX: both predicates were reading the TYPE, and what decides ownership is what the consuming READ did. MIR settles that at the use site (`placeOperand`): non-Copy MOVES, a Copy value composite DUPLICATES into an independent clone, and both leave the compare owning something. So the decision is now a named three-way classification — `scrutineeBorrowed` / `scrutineeMoved` / `scrutineeDuplicated` (`compareScrutineeOwnership`) — and both halves hang off it: a duplicated scrutinee may be released even though it is Copy and even through a deref (freeing a clone cannot disturb the original), and its arms must release DEEP, because the arm's binding clones AGAIN rather than taking the payload out, so a shallow envelope free would abandon the payload box. `scrutineeReadsThroughBorrow` stops being an unconditional veto and disqualifies only the MOVING read, which is the genuine move-out-of-a-borrow. INDEPENDENT REVIEW confirmed both halves and supplied a stronger argument for the second than the one used: a valid `@copy` union CANNOT contain a move-only member (recursively enforced, `internal/sema/attr_validation_types.go`), so no arm can have taken anything out of the clone and a deep drop cannot double-free. It also noted that `scrutineeDuplicated` is not literally true of every rvalue — MIR may MOVE an already-owned temporary — so the name describes the required reclamation strategy rather than the actual transfer; safe either way, since both mean the compare owns it. Measured: all three shapes go to strict zero with 0 memcheck errors, and the all-bound and whole-value-binding arms stay at zero. PINNED, not just measured: two rows joined the reclamation census (`composite-bound`, `composite-wildcard`), whose negative control is reverting this fix — it then exits 91 on the `composite-bound` differential. | Closed | Compare scrutinee release (HIR normalize) | `compare` over a borrowed or Copy value-composite scrutinee reports strict-zero definitely-lost; contract row 4 joins the reclamation census; `TestRuntimeV2CompareScrutineeReleaseCensusBalanced` and the union-payload independence row stay green. |
| RV2-DEBT-010 | Copied net handles carried only raw native fd/pointer-shaped state: public data-path ops could validate stale in-struct `closed` state before raw syscalls, and reconstructed `{ __opaque = handle }` boxes made reads past the public handle word unsafe. | Epic 10 Task 3 | CLOSED by the stable runtime handle-id contract (`10-tasks/03-debt-010-net-handle-contract.md`, commit `9d1b06c1`). `TcpConn.__opaque` and `TcpListener.__opaque` now carry monotonic runtime handle ids, not OS fds or pointers. Native entrypoints canonicalize through the handle table before reading fd, owner shard, closed state, or fd-registry generation; closed conn/listener handles are removed from the table and stale copies return `NET_ERR_NOT_CONNECTED`. Live canonical conn operations also validate owner-local execution and the owner shard's fd-registry generation. Proof: `TestRuntimeV2NetHandleStaleCopyReusedFD` (`SURGE_SHARDS=1,2,8`) + `TestRuntimeV2NetHandleGuardStaticShape`, wired as `runtime-v2-net-handle-check`; `make runtime-v2-net-handle-check`, `make runtime-v2-accept-check`, `make c-check`, and commit-hook `make check` passed. Residual boundary: handle ids are intentionally not reused and the handle table grows monotonically until a future Surge-visible net-object destructor exists. |
| RV2-DEBT-079 | Ownership of an OWNED crossing capture across the transport edge is not settled, and the two sides disagree in a way neither side's comments admit. Discovered 2026-07-28 while specifying Epic 24 step 0 (the generated transfer protocol). `rewriteSpawnOnPollReturns` (`internal/mir/lower_expr_crossing_spawn_poll.go`) synthesizes a drop for COPY captures only, stating that an owned one may be CONSUMED by the body and a synthesized drop cannot see that; the body's unpacked local carries no scope-exit obligation (sema knows the symbol as a binding of the ENCLOSING function); and the envelope release is shallow by construction. By that reading an owned capture the body reads but never hands on is abandoned. MEASURED: it is NOT. A `@shard_movable` struct with a 200-character string field, moved into `spawn on distributed { ret j.id * 100 + 6; }` (no allocation inside the body, so nothing else can be attributed), reports `definitely lost: 0` over 8 crossings on the native backend, both before and after the Epic 24 step-0 fix. So SOMETHING reclaims it, and it is not any of the three sites above. The converse is proven too: registering the capture as an obligation of the crossing body — the obvious fix, and the one an adversarial review recommended — turns the program into an INVALID READ plus an INVALID FREE inside the body's drop glue, so the body is demonstrably not the only holder. One further observation, recorded as UNVERIFIED because the measurement that produced it is the confounded one: the caller emits its own `drop` for the moved binding at the enclosing scope's end (that drop is present in the MIR both before and after the Epic 24 step-0 fix, so it is not new), and it is not established what that drop actually reclaims. Re-measure before relying on it. CONSEQUENCE for Epic 24: step 0 cannot state the generated protocol as a checkable invariant until this is resolved — the invariant would assert an ownership fact that no site in the compiler actually implements, and a MIR verifier written on top of it would be decorative. Also blocks the wider fix an adversarial review asked for (an explicit `FieldReadMoveOut` vs `FieldReadCopy` mode on `FieldAccess`, since `RValueField` today carries no transfer mode and its object operand is always `OperandCopy`). NOT a live correctness hazard at HEAD: no leak, no invalid access, on either backend, in the shapes measured. ANSWERED 2026-07-28, same session: the reclaiming site is the CALLER's own scope-exit drop of the captured binding, proven by valgrind's free'd-at stack (the second free is `drop.type1502` in the crossing poll; the FIRST is the same glue called from the caller's poll). That drop exists because sema never marks a crossing capture as moved — `observeMove` is called for `blocking` captures and for far-task await, but NOT for `on`/`spawn on` captures — so the caller keeps the binding live and drops it at scope end. That is why nothing leaks and why a body-side drop double-frees. It is safe today only because the affine must-await lifecycle keeps the handle's await inside the capture's scope, which orders the caller's drop after the body's last read. The remaining question is no longer 'what frees it' but whether the caller SHOULD be the owner at all, which is RV2-DEBT-081. CLOSED 2026-07-28 by Epic 24 step 0b, which moved the ownership rather than the drop: the capture is now a real move (RV2-DEBT-081), so the caller stops dropping it and the crossing body starts. Both halves had to change together — either alone is a double free or a leak, which is exactly why the first attempt failed. Proven by valgrind across eight shapes: unconsumed capture, consumed capture, mixed-branch (one exit hands the value on, the other does not), body-local live at `ret`, loop and non-loop, the immediate `on` form, and a CANCELLED far task whose body never runs. Zero definitely-lost, zero invalid reads/writes/frees in all eight. | Closed (ownership moved to the crossing body; step 0b) | Crossing capture ownership (sema + mir lowering) | The reclaiming site for an owned capture is IDENTIFIED and named in one place; the crossing capture protocol is stated as an invariant a test asserts; a body that neither consumes nor hands on an owned capture is proven leak-free and double-free-free by allocation census plus valgrind on both backends, with a consuming body as the negative control. |
| RV2-DEBT-080 | A `blocking { }` body abandons a constant 219 bytes, INDEPENDENT of iteration count — 1 block at n=1 and 1 block at n=8, so it is not a per-execution leak and NOT the same mechanism as the crossing `ret` defect that Epic 24 step 0 fixed. Measured 2026-07-28 on the native backend with a `@shard_movable` struct carrying a 200-character string moved into a `blocking` body that reads it and returns an int. Blocking bodies still have all drop-obligation recording suppressed in `dropObligationsSuppressed` (`internal/sema/drop_obligations.go`); crossings had the same suppression lifted when RV2-DEBT-034 was found to have closed, but blocking was deliberately left alone because its release path is a separate shallow free on the pool side (`runtime/native/rt_async_blocking.c`) and was not probed. So the suppression there is a PARKED question, not a proven boundary: it may be hiding the same class of defect the crossing side had, and the constant-size loss above is a hint that something else is also wrong. Unprobed: whether the loss scales with the number of distinct blocking sites rather than executions, and whether it is a pool-shutdown artifact. | Open | Blocking body drop obligations (sema suppression + pool-side release) | The 219-byte loss is attributed to a named site; blocking bodies either record drop obligations like crossing bodies or the suppression is justified by a stated ownership contract that a test asserts; a blocking body that builds and holds a droppable at its exit is proven leak-free by allocation census plus valgrind. |
| RV2-DEBT-081 | Moving an owned value into an `on` / `spawn on` crossing is NOT A MOVE for the caller, so the ownership rule the crossing surface advertises is not enforced. `checkOnCaptures` classifies a capture as `CrossingCaptureMoveOwned` and the MIR unpacking treats it as transferred, but no site calls `observeMove` on the capture expression (`internal/sema/on_crossing_capture.go`; contrast `typeExprBlocking` at `internal/sema/type_expr_flow.go`, which does). Measured 2026-07-28, native backend, all accepted with NO diagnostic: (a) reading the binding after the crossing; (b) MUTATING it — `j.id = 99` after `spawn on distributed { ret j.id * 100 + 6; }` prints caller 99 / body 406, so the two sides disagree about the same value; (c) MOVING IT AGAIN — `consume(own j)` after the capture compiles, i.e. one owned value is moved twice. Consequence beyond the missing diagnostic: replacing a captured heap field in the caller (`j.note = build_note("z-")`) ABANDONS 219 bytes, because the shipped state still references the old string and nothing frees it. No use-after-free was produced in the shapes measured — the affine must-await rule keeps the await inside the capture's scope, which orders the caller's drop after the body's last read, and attempts to defer the await past that scope are rejected by the must-await-or-return check. So the current safety rests on a lifecycle rule rather than on ownership. This is the same defect class Epic 24 exists to fix (an ownership rule that sema does not track), found while resolving RV2-DEBT-079, and it decides that row: if the capture becomes a real move, the caller stops dropping it and the BODY must, which is exactly the change that double-frees today. CLOSED 2026-07-28, same session it was opened, by Epic 24 step 0b. `checkOnCaptures` now calls `observeMove` for a `CrossingCaptureMoveOwned` capture, and `registerCrossingBodyOwnership` gives the body the drop obligation the caller just gave up. Reading the binding after the capture, moving it a second time, and capturing the same owned value into TWO crossings are all rejected as use-after-move; a Copy capture stays usable and an owned capture nobody touches afterwards stays accepted (`internal/sema/crossing_capture_move_test.go`). Far-handle captures are deliberately untouched: the anchored form USES the destination handle inside the body it was captured for, so consuming it would reject every anchored channel operation. RESIDUAL, and it is NOT crossing-specific: a field WRITE after a move is still unchecked — `j.id = 99` after the capture is accepted, exactly as `consume(own j); j.note = "z";` is accepted with no crossing in sight, because the moved-set is symbol-keyed and the write goes through a projection. Epic 24's own steps 3 and 5 close that, and `TestCrossingCaptureFieldWriteIsNotYetCaught` is the tripwire that fails when they do. | Closed (residual field-write gap owned by Epic 24 steps 3/5) | Crossing capture move tracking (sema) | A capture classified `CrossingCaptureMoveOwned` marks the binding moved; a later read, mutation or second move reports use-after-move; whichever side then owns the value is stated once and proven by allocation census plus valgrind on both backends, with a consuming body and a mutating caller as controls. |
| RV2-DEBT-082 | A FAR-HANDLE capture crossing an `on` boundary has no stated owner, and the two layers disagree. MIR lowers every capture as a consuming read into the shipped state (`internal/mir/lower_expr_crossing.go`), but sema leaves the caller's binding live: Epic 24 step 0b marked `CrossingCaptureMoveOwned` captures moved and deliberately left `CrossingCaptureMoveFarHandle` alone, because the ANCHORED form uses the destination handle INSIDE the body it was captured for (`on ch { ch.send(1); ... }`), so consuming it would reject every anchored channel operation — pinned by the `anchored_far_channel_still_usable` row in `internal/sema/crossing_capture_move_test.go`. That exclusion is a workaround for symbol-granular move tracking, not an ownership model. As it stands the handle is either TWO owners (if the transport is a move, which the lowering says it is) or a hidden lease (if the transport shares, which nothing states). Raised by adversarial review 2026-07-28: the comment claiming the lease travels with the handle is an assertion, not an implementation. Candidate resolutions: a distinct anchor/lease capture mode that is explicitly not a move; or marking the caller's binding moved AFTER body analysis while the body uses a separate anchor capability. Note the second becomes cheaper once Epic 24 makes move tracking place-granular. No leak or invalid access has been measured on this path — `share()` leases (Epic 16) and the affine handle lifecycle appear to cover it in practice — but nothing pins that, and no probe was written. | Open | Far-handle crossing captures (sema capture classification + mir lowering) | A far-handle capture has ONE stated owner, spelled in the capture mode rather than in a comment; the anchored form stays legal; and a far handle captured into a crossing is proven leak-free and double-free-free by allocation census plus valgrind, with an anchored-op program and a plain move-in program as the two controls. |
| RV2-DEBT-083 | A partial move whose result ESCAPES corrupts memory — it is not merely the alias RV2-DEBT-077 records. Two shapes measured 2026-07-28 on the native backend while landing Epic 24 step 1, both printing the RIGHT answer and exiting 0 while valgrind reports invalid reads and invalid frees: (a) a by-value receiver handing out a heap field, `fn get_bar(self: Foo) -> string { return self.bar; }` — seven distinct invalid-read contexts plus an invalid free over 8 calls; (b) a value built from the fields of one that dies immediately, `return Req { method = head.method, target = head.target };` — invalid reads plus TWO invalid frees. The string leaves with the escaping value while the container's drop takes it as well. The alias case RV2-DEBT-077 records is protected by `markAliasedBinding` (a projection-read binding never drops, leaking rather than double-freeing); neither shape above goes through that, because the value escapes by return or into a literal rather than staying in a binding. The borrowed receiver `self: &Foo` is a TYPE ERROR (`expected string, got &string`), so the damage is confined to by-value receivers and struct-literal field reads. MITIGATED, not fixed, by Epic 24 step 1: the gate rejects both shapes, so they are unreachable from source; the 8 stdlib sites that used them were rewritten to borrow at the use site or to copy explicitly (`stdlib/http/interim_copy.sg`, a file step 8 deletes). The underlying defect is the same one the epic exists to fix. CLOSED 2026-07-29 by Epic 24 step 8: both shapes compile with `own` written and are covered by the valgrind census; `stdlib/http/interim_copy.sg` is deleted and its six callers take their fields directly. | Closed (the escaping partial move is a real transfer) | Partial moves that escape (sema) | `own o.field` performs a real transfer, the container is invalidated for that place only, and each shape above is proven leak-free and double-free-free by allocation census plus valgrind — with the interim copies in `stdlib/http` deleted and the direct field reads restored. |
| RV2-DEBT-084 | A field read whose BASE is a TEMPORARY segfaults. `let e = mk().inner;` where `Inner` owns heap crashes on the native backend; under valgrind it is invalid reads plus invalid frees. The temporary is materialized, the binding takes a projection of it, and the temporary's statement-end drop frees what the binding now holds. Measured 2026-07-28 at Epic 24 step 1, PRE-EXISTING (identical with the step-1 gate reverted). NOT gated by step 1, and that is a gap rather than a decision: `resolvePlace` does not resolve a call as a place, so the rejection never sees it. This is the shape the epic's contract row 11 and trap 3 both require to STAY ACCEPTED, on the reasoning that the base is a temporary nobody else can be using — which is correct as an ownership argument and is exactly why it should WORK. As it stands, 'stays accepted' means 'accepts a use-after-free'. Epic 23's owning-temp marking (`consumeTempCandidate`) is called on this path and does not cover it. Pinned as a sentinel by `TestPartialMoveOutOfTemporaryIsNotGated`. MITIGATED 2026-07-29, not fixed: the shape is now REFUSED (`SemaPartialMoveUnsupported`) rather than compiled into a crash, so the segfault is unreachable from source. Blast radius measured before gating — ONE site in the whole corpus (`s.bytes().len`, showcase 13), and that one is a false positive of the probe rather than a real move, so the corpus is byte-identical with the gate in. Deliberately NOT fixed here: the MIR shows the fix is step 6's mechanism applied to a temporary rather than to a named binding — `drop L2` must release the temporary's RESIDUAL instead of all of it, exactly as a partially-moved binding must. Fixing it earlier means building a special case that steps 6 and 7 then replace. It does NOT block steps 6/7 (it consumes them); it DOES block step 8, whose contract row 11 requires `f().inner` to stay accepted — which today would mean accepting a use-after-free. STILL OPEN after Epic 24 step 8, and deliberately: the rest of step 8 landed for NAMED bindings and the temporary did not. The reason is sharper than the row's original guess. Steps 6+7 give a residual drop to a value with a NAME, computed from that symbol's moved-set; a temporary has no symbol, so `TempDrops` would have to carry a plan the way `DropLocal` now does and sema would have to compute a residual from an expression's TYPE plus the projections taken out of it. That is a separable piece of work, not a tail of steps 6/7. The refusal moved to its own code (`SEM3179`) so it is not confused with the two rules that are permanent, and it is SAFE: a compile error naming the way around it, not a miscompile. Contract row 11 is therefore NOT satisfied by step 8. CLOSED 2026-07-29, later the same day, once the residual walk stopped being about symbols. `residualProbe` separates the question (what does the moved-set say about this path) from the walk, so a binding answers from its interned moved-set and a temporary answers from the paths taken out of it; `TempDrops` carries a plan the way `DropLocal` does and MIR's temp frames carry it to the flush. The plan is COMPLETE for a nameless value because a temporary cannot outlive its statement or be mentioned again, so it is computed when the temp frame closes. MIR now shows `drop L2.label; drop_shallow L2` where it showed `drop L2`. Gated by the `from_temporary` row of `TestRuntimeV2PartialMoveReclaimsOnlyWhatItStillHolds` (valgrind, native, strict zero) plus a sema trio: bare, `own`-written, and a nested path all accepted, with an element off a temporary still refused. Contract row 11 now means accepted AND correct. SEM3179 survives, narrowed to a projection off a value whose release is not ours to narrow — a control-flow expression's result — where refusing is the conservative answer. | Closed (a temporary releases its remainder, like any other value) | Partial move out of a temporary (sema temp ownership + mir temp drops) | `let e = f().inner;` compiles, runs, and reports strict-zero definitely-lost with no invalid access on both backends; contract row 11 is restated as 'accepted AND correct' and pinned by valgrind rather than by compiling. |
| RV2-DEBT-085 | Eleven `TestLLVMParity` http rows fail to BUILD, not to run: `member "next" of module "stdlib/http" is not public or not a function`. `next` is declared `pub fn next(self: &mut ByteStream) -> Option<byte[]>` inside `extern<ByteStream> { ... }` (`stdlib/http/parser.sg`), so a `pub` method in an extern block is apparently not reachable as a module member the way the parity programs reference it. Noticed 2026-07-28 while looking for an acceptance test for the Epic 24 step-1 stdlib rewrites, and PRE-EXISTING (identical with every Epic 24 change reverted). Consequence for other work: the whole http parity suite is dark, so no end-to-end test currently covers `stdlib/http` — which is exactly where RV2-DEBT-083's memory corruption lived undetected. Any change to `stdlib/http` is presently unverifiable beyond compiling and hand-written valgrind probes. Affected rows: http_parse_request, http_parse_request_strict, http_response_bytes, http_response_formats, http_context_helpers, http_helpers, http_cookie_helpers, http_query_helpers, http_chunked_request, http_server, http_connect. Out of Epic 24's scope; recorded because it is load-bearing for anyone trusting the http suite. MOSTLY CLOSED 2026-07-29, and it was one contradictory line. `publicModuleValueExports` deliberately falls back to receiver-bound exports when a module has no free function of that name — that fallback is what makes `http.next(&mut body)` work, a method called with an explicit receiver — and `moduleFunctionResult` then filtered receiver-bound exports out again, discarding exactly the candidates the helper had just selected. The filter was unreachable whenever free functions existed, so it only ever fired on the fallback it was defeating. Removing it restored the build for every affected row. The rows themselves then hit the Epic 24 step-1 gate on `req.body` / `req.path` — real partial moves in the test programs — rewritten to advance the body reader in place (`&mut req.body` off a mutable rebinding) or to copy the field until step 8 makes `own req.path` a real move. RESULT: 10 of the 11 rows build and pass. The eleventh, `http_connect`, fails at BASELINE with `cannot send 'listener' of @nosend type 'TcpListener' to spawn` — a separate language-rule question, unrelated to module exports or to partial moves, and left alone. So `stdlib/http` has end-to-end coverage again, which matters beyond this row: it is the coverage whose absence let RV2-DEBT-083's memory corruption live in the request parser unnoticed. | Closed for the export defect; `http_connect` remains on an unrelated @nosend rule | Module exports for extern-block methods (project/module resolution) | Done for the export bug. The residual is `http_connect`'s @nosend violation, which needs its own decision: either the test program is wrong or the rule is too strict. |
| RV2-DEBT-086 | `@drop o.inner` — an explicit drop of ONE place — is settled as legal by Epic 24 (settled decision 4) and sema is ready for it, but NEITHER BACKEND CAN PERFORM IT, so it stays rejected until Epic 24 step 7. Measured 2026-07-29 by implementing it at step 5 and then backing it out: on the native backend a projected `InstrDrop` is a SILENT NO-OP (`placeBaseType` refuses a projection and `emitInstrDrop` swallows the error), so `@drop o.inner` releases nothing and the field is reclaimed later by the binding's ordinary scope-exit drop — valgrind clean, but the statement did not do what it says. On the VM it is worse: `execInstrDrop` IGNORES the projection and drops the whole local, so `@drop o.inner; return o.label;` panics `VM3301: use-after-free: local \"o\" used after drop` on the very next line. One statement, doing nothing on one backend and corrupting on the other. Sema now refuses it with a diagnostic naming the real reason, replacing `drop target must be a binding` — which was true of nothing and sent the reader after an addressability problem that did not exist. BLOCKED ON, not merely waiting for, step 7: the projected drop has to arrive together with step 6's path-carrying drop obligations, or the explicit drop and the binding's scope-exit drop both release the same field. CLOSED 2026-07-29 by Epic 24 step 8. `@drop o.inner` is an explicit move into nothing: it records the place as moved, so a later read of the field or of the container is a use-after-move and the sibling stays readable, and the container's exit drop reclaims the residual. Gated by the `explicit_drop` row of `TestRuntimeV2PartialMoveReclaimsOnlyWhatItStillHolds` (valgrind, strict zero) plus a sema pair: the sibling read is accepted, the dropped place is not. | Closed (a projected drop releases that place and only that place) | Explicit projected drop (sema surface + both backends) | `@drop o.inner` releases that field and only that field, the container's residual drops exactly once, and both are proven by allocation census plus valgrind on both backends — with a sibling read after the drop as the must-still-work control. |

Gates: `git diff --check`, `make c-check`, `make cppcheck`, `make runtime-v2-check`, `make check`, `./check_file_sizes.sh -a`, `sentrux check` root `6179` / runtime `5298` / native `5399` all-rules-pass. |

| RV2-DEBT-021 | No deterministic cross-owner scope-completion regression test. The cross-owner `scope_on_child_done` control-fallback path (`rt_async_scope.c:340-377`, Epic 8 Task 9) is the sole producer of the residual `ctrl_scope`=19464 (~2.375/req) on the 8-shard/1024 net benchmark; before this it was exercised only by that benchmark and the no-keepalive completion-pin TSan stress, neither deterministic in CI. The `Scope*AcrossShards` tests keep scope children same-owner and never take the path. | Epic 8 Task 14 closeout | CLOSED by `TestRuntimeV2LifecycleScopeCrossOwnerChildDone` (`internal/vm/runtime_v2_lifecycle_behavior_scope_test.go` + driver in `runtime_v2_lifecycle_behavior_placement_adoption_test.go`), wired into `runtime-v2-lifecycle-check`. It uses the REAL F2 machinery deterministically: a scope owner pinned to shard 0 registers a scope-child while same-owner, then parks in `join_all`; the scope-child adopts a shard-1 CONNECTION placement by consuming a connection-placed grandchild through `rt_task_poll_adopt_placement` (the production F2 path), so on completion `owner_shard_id` (1) != the scope's pinned shard (0) and `scope_on_child_done` takes the counted cross-owner control fallback, waking the owner cross-shard (the harness holds the owner parked until then, so the wake — not a self-consume — completes it). The driver asserts the child adopted the cross-owner shard, so the branch is non-vacuously exercised. Swept `SURGE_SHARDS=2,8` (SHARDS=1 clamps the grandchild to shard 0 → same-owner, path unreachable — documented). Passed 3/3 at both shard counts standalone. |
