(2b, the real root of 2, found by the no-keepalive stress) `wake_task_on_shard_locked`/`wake_task_with_policy` read and cleared a task's `park_key`/`park_prepared` BEFORE checking status, racing the JOINER's own unlocked register-then-commit writes (`prepare_park` via `rt_task_poll`, `park_current` via `apply_poll_outcome`) — closed by gating the wake path's `park_key` work on the task being parked (WAITING and not enqueued) under the owner shard lock; the wake token still fires unconditionally so a mid-park task aborts and re-runs. | Epic 8 Task 8 (+ Task 7 result reorder) | Baseline pre-existence confirmed: no-keepalive pin at clean `585e3c5c`, `SURGE_SHARDS=8` FAILED under TSan (exit 1, `tsan_warnings=1`). After the fix: `TestRuntimeV2LifecycleCompletionPinInterleavingTSan` (no-keepalive) PASSES under TSan at `SURGE_SHARDS=1,2,8`, `tsan_warnings=0`; `t.Skip` deleted and the test added to `runtime-v2-lifecycle-check`. Sibling debt RV2-DEBT-020 recorded the migrate path's higher-level (non-race) assumption and was later closed by Epic 9 Task 3. |

| RV2-DEBT-015 | 8-shard/1024-conn/100-req starvation: ~10.6-13.6s request tails healing on load drop, `Recv-Q=1` while 7 pollers idle and ready tasks sat in inject. | Epic 8 Task 11 (investigation) + Task 7 (F2 fix, `d998df20`) | Mechanism trace-pinned, not platform: a placement funnel (stdlib net ops are ephemeral wrapper child tasks; the accept transition placed the wrapper, so every durable task inherited shard 0) put ALL execution on one worker, and parked-read completions rotated through that shard's single-consumer inject FIFO (sojourn = backlog/drain — a deterministic ~1s band at full 1024-way concurrency; the historical >10s tails were the same rotation stretched by WSL2 host load, healing on load drop; a pre-fix build at `072bbde0` showed the identical band, ruling out the Task 1 lost-wake). Fixed by F2 placement adoption at join consume (`rt_task_poll_adopt_placement`, folded into Task 7). Quiet-window acceptance at `d998df20`: 90s sustained 8x1024 run with ZERO >=1s stalls (was 8.4% of requests) at +41% throughput, 8-shard 1.12x the 1-shard row (was 0.82x), balanced per-worker CPU, bench probe 5/5 clean with no >10s tails, owner histogram spread across all 8 shards, `inject_len=0`. Full log: `08-tasks/11-net-fairness-starvation-investigation.md`. |
| RV2-DEBT-087 | A compare-arm payload binding never acquires a drop obligation, so a heap payload bound and used locally is abandoned. Repro is six lines: `tag Bytes(byte[]); type Payload = Empty() \| Bytes(byte[]);` then `compare body { Bytes(b) => b.__len() to int; ... }` over an owned scrutinee loses one block per evaluation (96 bytes in 4 blocks over 4 iterations, valgrind, native). Measured 2026-07-29 while landing Epic 24 step 8, and PRE-EXISTING — byte-identical on the tree with every step-8 change reverted, so it is not a partial-move defect. It surfaced only because step 8 removed an accidental cover: the arm used to be followed by a whole drop of the container the scrutinee came from, and that drop walked into the union and freed the payload. Removing that drop was correct (it was a double free against the container's own residual drop — see the step-8 record), and it left the payload with no owner at all. This is RV2-DEBT-058's shape re-measured on a fresh path and adjacent to RV2-DEBT-052/075/078, all in the compare-arm release model, which Epic 24 lists as out of scope. Not a correctness hazard beyond the leak: no invalid read or free on either backend. The Epic 24 census row works around it by handing the payload to a sink that takes ownership, which keeps that row measuring the double free it exists for. CLOSED 2026-07-30. Two changes, and the second only became visible by measuring. (a) An arm's pattern bindings now get the scope-exit obligation every other binding has: a drop scope opens before the pattern is typed so the bindings land in it, `registerComparePayloadDroppables` registers them, and what the arm still holds is read off that scope before it closes — a payload the arm moved onward is skipped by liveDroppables, which is why the ubiquitous `Success(x) => x` shape hid this for so long. They ride the channel the one-sided arm obligations already use, so an arm can owe both. (b) `wrapArmDrops` emitted those drops BEFORE the arm's value, which was fine for the obligation it was written for (a binding a sibling arm moved, which the result never touches) and fatal for this one: an arm like `Bytes(b) => b.__len()` reads the very binding it owes, so the payload was freed and then read — an invalid read of a null slot, measured under valgrind. The drops now ride the `ret` as DropsAfterValue, which is the contract `return` already carries for exactly this reason. PROOF: the six-line repro goes from 96 bytes in 4 blocks to `in use at exit: 0 bytes in 0 blocks`, 0 memcheck errors; the Epic 24 census row that had been dodging this by handing its payload to a sink now binds and reads it directly, so it covers the leak and the double free together. Golden moved by exactly one line pair — an arm's `drop L4` after the arm's value instead of before, the only place in the corpus where an arm carried a drop — stable across two regenerations. What this unblocks: RV2-DEBT-052's recorded residuals (mixed payload arms and the whole-value binding arm errred toward leaking because 'without binding-level obligations there is no safe owner to hand the box to'), and `tagPayloadReleaseShape`'s shallow-envelope premise, which assumed the binding would drop and was until now simply false. RV2-DEBT-058 is the same row found earlier from the other side. | Closed (an arm's payload binding drops after the arm's value) | Compare-arm payload bindings (sema drop obligations) | An arm-bound heap payload used locally reclaims at arm end; the Epic 24 census row can drop its `sink_bytes` workaround and bind the payload plainly; RV2-DEBT-058's residual arm shapes are revisited with it. |
| RV2-DEBT-088 | `ret` carries a drop list that three maintenance sites never learned about, all of them the `return` counterpart's neighbours. `cloneStmt` (internal/mono/clone.go) did not copy `RetData.DropsAfterValue`, so every instantiation of a generic function shared ONE list — and `ApplyStmt` writes type substitutions into it, so the last instantiation would decide which drop glue all of them use; `ApplyStmt` (internal/mono/subst_apply.go) never substituted the drop TYPES at all, which is what picks the glue; and `remapStmt` (internal/driver/hir_stdlib.go) did not remap the drop SYMBOLS, so an imported `ret` would drop whatever now occupies those slots. `StmtReturn` does all three at each site; `ret` acquired its drop list later, at Epic 24 step 0b, and these were missed. Found 2026-07-29 by the Epic 24 step-9 audit, PRE-EXISTING, and LATENT rather than live: reaching any of them from source needs a non-empty drop list on a `ret` inside a generic or imported function, and the two shapes that produce one resist combining — a value-producing block expression records no drops at all (its locals leak by design, the recorded safe-direction bound in `appendBlockExprEndDrops`; measured at 392 bytes over 8 iterations and identical on the pre-epic tree), and a crossing body records them but could not be given a type-parameter-typed local. FIXED in the same pass, all three sites now mirroring their `return` neighbour. Gate: `internal/mono/ownership_metadata_test.go` asserts clone and substitution directly for every payload that carries ownership information, with `return` as the row that must always pass (it is what says the test measures a real difference) and negative controls that fire on each half of the `ret` gap separately. The stdlib remap half is fixed by inspection against the line above it — a `ret` does exist in `stdlib/bytes` (`peek_line_lf`) but its locals are Copy, so its list is empty and no measurement can distinguish the fix today. | Closed (all three sites mirror `return`) | Exit drop lists for `ret` (mono clone/subst, stdlib HIR import) | Done: a generic function's `ret` drop list is per-instantiation and type-substituted, an imported `ret`'s drop symbols are remapped, and a unit gate fails if a field is added to any ownership payload and these functions are not updated. |
| RV2-DEBT-090 | An index expression whose `__index` takes `self` BY VALUE reported itself as a use-after-move. The receiver is consumed while the expression is being typed, and the projection read check ran afterwards — so it found the move this expression had just made and rejected `b[0]` outright, on a program with no second read anywhere. `let b = Box { value = 1 }; return b[[1..2]];` went from compiling on main to `SEM3130 cannot use b[?]: b was moved`. Introduced 2026-07-29 by Epic 24 step 3 (`504fcb1c`, which added the place-aware check), found 2026-07-29 chasing golden. FIXED by asking the question BEFORE the expression is typed, which is also the order evaluation has: the place is read at that point, and whatever the operation then does with it happens after. Negative control run: a genuine use-after-move on an index base (`consume(b); b[0]`) still reports. Second half of the same row: `checkPlaceUseAfterMove` routed a WHOLE-binding move through the place-aware wording whenever the READ was a projection, so `b[0]` after `b` went entirely explained a partial move that had not happened; it now keys on the move alone, which is what step 3's own note said it should do. Golden rows restored: `hir/indexing_ranges`, `sema/valid/range_literals`, `sema/invalid/index_range_missing_overload`. | Closed | Place-aware use-after-move ordering (sema) | Done: an index whose operator consumes its receiver compiles, a real use-after-move on the same shape still reports, and a whole-binding move is worded as one. |
| RV2-DEBT-091 | Taking an element out of a TUPLE was refused alongside array elements, which left ordinary code with no spelling at all. Variant A's reasoning for refusing elements is that the survivors of one leaving cannot be listed — true of an array, whose index is chosen at runtime over a container whose length is not part of the question, and NOT true of a tuple, whose arity is fixed and which is only ever indexed by a literal. A tuple is a struct with positional names. Because `resolvePlace` mapped both to one segment kind, Epic 24 step 1 (`12e8dac6`) refused both, and step 8 kept the refusal while lifting it for struct fields — so `let s: string = t.1;` was rejected as a move AND had no accepted form, making a tuple holding anything move-only unreadable. Measured against main: `let inner: (int, int) = nested.0;` and `return t.1;` both compiled there. FIXED: a tuple element is its own place segment carrying its position (`PlaceSegmentTupleIndex`), interned distinctly, enumerable in the residual walk beside struct fields, and projected in MIR as the positional field the rest of the lowering already uses. Two plumbing gaps closed with it: the residual plan silently dropped the tuple projection and emitted a drop of the WHOLE container, and the tuple read never carried the move-out mode so a taken element was counted as a second holder. Also found here: `own T` was accepted for `T` only when the ids matched, so a structurally identical but separately interned tuple made the marker look like a type error (the RV2-DEBT-057 class); it now asks assignability. Gate: two rows in `TestRuntimeV2PartialMoveReclaimsOnlyWhatItStillHolds` (a tuple element with its sibling surviving, and a path mixing field and element), valgrind strict zero; contract rows for accept, sibling survival, mixed path, and the use-after-move control, with the ARRAY refusal kept as the row that says the two are not the same. | Closed | Tuple element as an enumerable place (sema/hir/mir) | Done: a tuple element can be taken with `own`, its sibling survives, its residual is reclaimed exactly once on both backends, and an array element stays refused with the reason that actually applies to it. |
| RV2-DEBT-182 | **CLOSED 2026-08-09 in the same change that found it.** Nine diagnostic numbers were claimed by two different diagnostics each, so nine distinct errors rendered as the same `SEMxxxx` id. `Code.ID()` (`internal/diag/codes.go:635`) is purely range-based, so the number IS the identity a user sees, the key `Title()` looks a description up by, and the seed `fix.MakeFixID` derives a quick-fix identity from; the second claimant answered for the first at all three at once. Eight were the ownership block `codes.go` added at 3136-3143 (2026-07-14) landing on the crossing block `codes_crossing.go` had reserved at 3136-3143 (2026-07-07); the ninth was `SemaStoreThroughSharedRef` (`aecd98d3`) landing on `SemaAliasMagicRedeclared` (`d757ac61`), two lanes independently taking the next free number 3187. The golden corpus recorded BOTH readings of the same id — `sema/invalid/borrow_into_owned_method_param.diag` and `crossing/block01/invalid/far_negative_remote_own.diag` both assert SEM3137 — and nothing objected. WHY NOTHING OBJECTED, which is the part worth keeping: duplicate constant keys in a map literal are already a compile error in Go, so the one table in `codes.go` was self-policing all along; the five sibling code files defeated it by merging their descriptions in through `init()` at run time instead of writing them into the literal, and a map assignment silently overwrites. The split into sibling files was itself done to keep `codes.go` under the LOC ceiling, so a soft style limit bought a real defect class. | Closed | Diagnostics registry | Done, and the guarantee is the compiler rather than a test: every description now lives in the single `codeDescription` literal, the five `init()` merges are deleted, and reintroducing a collision fails the build with `duplicate key 3187 in map literal` (verified by reintroducing it). Two tests keep that reachable — `TestEveryDiagnosticCodeHasADescriptionEntry` refuses a code with no entry, since an unlisted code is one the literal cannot police, and `TestEveryDiagnosticCodeNumberIsClaimedOnce` reads the declarations out of the sources to name both claimants and the highest number in use. Both were proven by breaking them. The crossing block moved to 3188-3195 and the alias-magic code to 3196, because `codes.go` is the registry of record and the claimant outside it yields. Standing rule: new codes are declared in `codes.go`; if the LOC gate objects, `codes.go` goes on its exception list rather than the codes going into a new file. |
| RV2-DEBT-183 | The VM allocates a QUADRATIC number of heap objects in the number of await sites a coroutine has. Measured on the pinned compiler 2026-08-10, one run per point and stable across five repeats at n=2: n=0 -> 10, n=1 -> 17, n=2 -> 26, n=3 -> 37, n=4 -> 50, n=5 -> 65, which is `allocations = n^2 + 6n + 10`. The second difference is exactly 2, so each additional await site makes EVERY suspension cost two more allocations — the per-suspension cost is proportional to the number of state-machine variants rather than to the live locals a suspension actually carries, which is what a suspension should cost. The program measured is the `async_no_poll_alloc` fixture shape: `rt_heap_stats()`, an async block with n `checkpoint().await()` calls, `rt_heap_stats()` again. Nothing leaks and nothing is retained: live_blocks after the await is 8 at n=0, 2 and 4 alike, and the residue is the HeapStats struct plus the awaited `Task<int>` handle. Found while triaging the behavioural runners after they were made to execute (RV2-DEBT-173); it is the reason that fixture kept a stale budget of 15 for seven months without anyone noticing the real number had reached 26. This is a performance defect, not a correctness one, and it is deliberately NOT a reason to leave the gate red: the recorded budget is now 26, the true value, and it will change again when this is fixed — which is what a budget is for. | Open | Runtime V2, async lowering; own work item | The per-suspension allocation cost is independent of how many OTHER await sites the coroutine has, so allocations grow linearly in n rather than quadratically. Evidence is the same six measurements repeated with a first difference that does not grow, plus the `async_no_poll_alloc` budget re-recorded from at least three agreeing runs in the same commit. Related: RV2-DEBT-174 removed the per-ABORTED-park pair of allocations from this same lowering, which is a different quantity — that one was charged per spurious wake, this one is charged per suspension per site. |
| RV2-DEBT-184 | The NATIVE backend refuses to compile eight programs the VM runs, so the behavioural corpus cannot be green on both backends. Two distinct emit gaps, found 2026-08-10 by running the corpus on the native lane for the first time. (1) `unknown external function` for a monomorphised intrinsic the emitter never declared — `__add::<uint>` in `strings_std`/`strings_rope_std` (`llvm emit levenshtein bb6 instr[4]`), and the same shape in `abi_string_bytesview`, `arrays_drop_nested`, `array_range_panics`, `range_for_in`, `strings_rope`. (2) `call has destination but returns void: fn.3` in `map_index_set` (`llvm emit main bb0 instr[2]`), where the call has a destination place but the emitted function signature returns nothing. Both are compile-time refusals: the program never runs, so this is not a wrong answer but an unimplemented one. | Open | Runtime V2 native backend | Each of the eight compiles and runs on `--backend=llvm` and produces the recorded `.out`, verified by `make behaviour-check-all` reporting no `/llvm` failures for them. Group the fix by emit gap rather than by fixture: one missing declaration path and one signature mismatch account for all eight. |
| RV2-DEBT-185 | The NATIVE backend gives three WRONG ANSWERS the VM gets right, each a different mechanism, all found 2026-08-10 when the behavioural corpus first ran on the native lane. (1) `vm_int8_overflow_add`: the VM traps the overflow and exits 1; native exits 0 with no output, so a program that must fail silently succeeds. (2) `arrays_view_push_panics`: where the recorded answer is `1`, `1`, `2`, native prints 300-digit garbage (`-79843148642387849365584091149302591932883272793223952679927584496378351251319912547917764444...`), which is a read of memory that holds something else. (3) `async_cancel_propagation`: native prints only `parent=cancelled` and loses `child=cancelled` — the SAME defect fixed on the VM side in this branch, where the poll asked "am I cancelled?" before "is the awaited task done?", so a cancelled awaiter unwound before collecting a result that was already waiting. The VM fix is the model; check whether the native poll has the same ordering. **(3) IS FIXED 2026-08-10.** The native poll did have that ordering — `rt_task_poll` answered a cancelled caller before asking the target anything — and the check is now made after the DONE delivery instead. It is moved rather than removed, because what it guards is the join-waiter registration below it. Measured at `SURGE_THREADS=1`: 0 correct out of 20 before, 20 out of 20 after. Recorded because it nearly went the other way: measuring the same change at the default worker count says the opposite, since none of the answers at 32 workers is promised. **(1) is a SEPARATE problem and stays open by owner ruling 2026-08-10: the native backend has NO overflow trap for fixed-width arithmetic at all** — the checks in `emit_intrinsics_numeric.go` cover `to` conversions only, so `a + b` on `int8` wraps silently where the VM raises VM1101. Note the fixture cannot go green on this row alone: its recorded answer is a panic WITH span and backtrace, so it also needs RV2-DEBT-187. | Open | Runtime V2 native backend | All three produce the recorded `.out` on `--backend=llvm`. The garbage read in (2) is the one to treat as urgent: printing another value's bytes is a memory defect, not a formatting one. |
| RV2-DEBT-186 | The NATIVE runtime terminates ABNORMALLY AND SILENTLY when a scope exits holding an unreclaimable reference cycle. `testdata/golden/vm_rc/vm_rc_leak_panics.sg` builds two arrays that point at each other; adding a `print` before `return` shows the body completes on both backends. The VM then reports `panic VM3302: heap leak detected: 2 objects still alive` and exits 1. Native exits **255 with empty stdout and empty stderr** — no message, no code, nothing to act on. Narrowed 2026-08-10: a borrow plus deref-copy alone, an `@drop` of the borrow alone, and a one-way assignment each exit 0 on native, so it takes the completed two-way cycle to trigger. Distinguish this from "native has no leak detector", which is true — `heap leak detected` exists only in `internal/vm/drop.go:186` — but is NOT what this row is about: a runtime with no detector should leak and exit 0, not die without a word. **MEASURED 2026-08-10, and the measurement removes most of the option space.** First, the crash is precise: `valgrind` reports `Stack overflow in thread #1 ... at drop.type1518`. The native drop glue is generated PER TYPE and memoised by `requireDropGlue`, so a recursive type produces one mutually recursive pair, `drop.typeN` and `drop_elem.typeN`, trampolining through `rt_array_free_elems`. The compiler's own cycle guard over the TYPE graph (`typeOwnsHeapRec`'s seen-set) is already correct; what runs away is the VALUE graph, for which the glue carries no object identity. Second, and decisively: a thread-local in-progress guard was written and tried at `array_free_base_storage`, and it DOES make the walk finite - the stack overflow disappears. What it exposes underneath is a double free, and the trace names the reason: `fn.1` drops BOTH arrays, and the walk started by dropping the first already frees the second, so the second drop reads a header that is gone. That is not a traversal defect. It is the program having TWO owners for each object, which is exactly what the ownership verifier already flags here as `OWN-001`/`OWN-002`. Native arrays carry no reference count at all - `rt_array_internal.h`'s header is `{len, cap, data}` - so no traversal order can arbitrate between two owners. THEREFORE: "reclaim the cycle" is unsound, "report a leak" has nothing to detect one with, and "leak and exit 0" cannot know which owner is redundant. The guard was reverted rather than kept, because its only effect is on a program the compiler already rejects and it costs a check on every array free. The remaining answers are (a) refuse the shape in the compiler - the check exists and already fires on these two lines under `--dev`, though promoting it interacts with the whole Epic 25 ownership-corpus subsystem and is a decision at that scale - or (b) give native arrays a reference count, which is a runtime-model change. | Open | Runtime V2 native runtime | Native either reclaims the cycle, or reports something a caller can act on, and exits deliberately. Whatever it does, it does not exit 255 in silence. Whether native grows a leak report at all is a separate question and may be answered No; this row is satisfied by an exit that is explained. |
| RV2-DEBT-187 | A native panic prints its message and nothing else, where the VM prints the message, the source span and a backtrace. `arrays_negative_index_panics` records five lines — `panic VM2105: array index -1 out of range for length 4`, `at testdata/.../arrays_negative_index_panics.sg:4:13`, `backtrace:`, and two frames — and native emits only the first. Same for `arrays_positive_index_panics` and `abi_arrays_views_panics`. **OWNER RULING 2026-08-10: the native backend must carry both, and traceability generally.** The reason is a planned language feature, `surge exec <binary>`, which runs a compiled binary in isolation and captures traces; a panic that names neither its location nor its call chain gives that feature nothing to capture. The open question is therefore not WHETHER but what it costs at run time — what has to be retained, and whether the cost is paid always or only under a flag. | Open | Runtime V2 native backend; owner ruling taken, cost model open | A native panic prints the same message, span and backtrace the VM prints, and the three fixtures pass on `--backend=llvm` unchanged. The run-time cost of retaining spans and frames is measured and recorded before the mechanism is chosen, since that cost is the only part still in question. **SPLIT 2026-08-10 by measurement, and the halves cost nothing alike.** The SPAN is already available at the emit site: `mir.Instr` carries no span, and the VM does not read one — `setSpanForInstr` (`internal/vm/vm_util.go:224`) recovers a location from the DECLARATION span of the instruction's destination local (`mir.Local.Span`, `internal/mir/types.go:69`), and the LLVM `funcEmitter` holds the same `*mir.Func` and the same `*mir.Instr`. It simply never reads it: `.Span` appears zero times in `internal/backend/llvm`. The emitted panic call already sits in a cold block terminated by `unreachable`, so widening it with a constant span pointer costs nothing on the live path. What is missing is only that `EmitModule` is not handed a `*source.FileSet`, so it cannot render `path:line:col`, and the `rt_panic*` family takes a message and nothing else. The BACKTRACE has no foundation: user functions are emitted as `fn.<ID>`, no `!dbg` metadata is produced, and the IR is compiled without `-g`, so the binary carries DWARF for the C runtime only. One fixture, `abi_arrays_views_panics`, panics from inside `rt_array.c` where no caller context exists at all — only a maintained call chain can reach it — and it additionally prints VM3202 where the VM records VM1003, which is a separate small correction. **OWNER RULING 2026-08-10: the static span ships as its own change, ahead of and independent of any decision about the call chain, and the chain's design is discussed separately.** |
| RV2-DEBT-188 | Eight behavioural fixtures interleave differently on the two backends, and NOBODY HAS DECIDED whether that is a defect or a licence. `t04_fairness` records `L1 R1 L2 R2 L3 R3` and native produces `L1 R1 L2 L3 R2 R3`; the values are identical and only the order differs. The same for `async_fairness`, `async_joiners`, `vm_async_j7_unbuffered`, `t08_joiners`, `t10a_timeout_cancel`, `t15_fairness_round_robin` and `t16_no_starvation_chatty`. This is the question `docs/RUNTIME_V2.md` (Refactor Policy) already told the project to answer BEFORE changing scheduling: "document which scheduler properties are language contracts and which are current implementation artifacts. The VM backend and golden tests may implicitly depend on FIFO ordering that the native runtime should not promise forever." Eight fixtures now depend on exactly that, and until the question is answered it cannot be said whether native is wrong or the fixtures over-specify. Owner has flagged this as needing discussion. **MEASURED 2026-08-10, and the row as filed conflates two things.** All eight fixtures match their recorded `.out` byte-for-byte on `--backend=llvm` at `SURGE_THREADS=1` — 20 runs each, zero failures — and diverge run-to-run at the default worker count, which is `max(2, nproc)` (32 on the measuring machine). So this is not a backend disagreement about ordering; it is the corpus running the native lane in a configuration nothing promises ordering for. `internal/vm/test_helpers_test.go` already has `envForParity`, which appends `SURGE_THREADS=1` and is used by every LLVM parity suite; the behavioural corpus is the one harness that does not use it. **OWNER RULING 2026-08-10, in two halves.** (1) Backend PARITY is compared single-threaded, because the VM is single-threaded and a multi-worker interleaving is not a backend difference. (2) That is NOT the whole test story: the native binary IS multithreaded, and the new native runtime must be exercised multithreaded as well, because a defect that only appears once work is split across workers is invisible to a single-worker lane. So the resolution is TWO lanes, not a narrowing to one. Also ruled: `docs/CONCURRENCY.md` states the language-level async rules of the CURRENT model and pairs with `docs/RUNTIME.md`, which describes the runtime that exists today; `docs/RUNTIME_V2.md` is a separate target document and says so in its own first line ("target architecture, not an implementation contract"). So CONCURRENCY.md's F1/F2/F3 are today's contract, not V2's, and they may not survive V2 unchanged. Any edit to any of the three is agreed with the owner first rather than made in passing. | Open | Language contract; owner ruling taken 2026-08-10 (two lanes); the MT lane's assertions still to be designed | The scheduler ordering properties that ARE language contracts are written down, in RUNTIME_V2.md or the spec, and the eight fixtures either pass on both backends or record only the properties the contract promises. Fixing native to match the VM without answering the question first is explicitly NOT the close condition — it would freeze an artifact into a promise. |
| RV2-DEBT-189 | A compare arm that gave away PART of its payload binding freed that part twice on the native backend. Eleven lines reproduce it with no array and no call: `type Foo = { name: string, n: int }`, `let v: Foo? = Some(Foo { name = "x", n = 1 })`, then `compare v { Some(x) => print(own x.name); _ => print("<nothing>"); }`. The VM prints `x` then `done` and exits 0; the native binary prints `x` and dies with `free(): double free detected in tcache 2`. The binding genuinely owns the payload — it is lowered as a move-out of the envelope — and `own x.name` gives the string away, but the arm then dropped the binding WHOLE and the generated glue freed the string the callee had already taken. CAUSE, one missing question: a compare arm's obligations come from `liveDroppables`, which excludes a binding only when it moved ENTIRELY, so a binding that lost one field is still owed a drop but a NARROWED one. Every sibling obligation channel — scope end, block-expression end, early exit — follows its drop list with `recordResidualDrops`; the compare-arm channel recorded its list and its one-sided plans and never asked for the residual, so the lowering looked up a plan that had never been written and emitted the un-narrowed drop. The plan has to be read INSIDE the arm loop: it is a question about that arm's moved-set, and the recording loop runs after the arms are merged, so asking there answers for the union. Two things about the shape are worth keeping. It is the same defect the OTHER branch channel already had — a moved place folded up to its whole binding — fixed there and never carried across when this channel was added. And it was invisible from the VM, which consumes the identical MIR and survives only because its move-out physically empties the slot (`takeMember` → `storageZero`), so a wrong obligation produced a right answer on one backend; the native backend never reads `MoveOut` at all. It was also invisible from the corpus until 2026-08-10, because the only fixture that reaches it, `arrays_drop_nested`, was refused at emit time for an unrelated missing symbol and never ran that far. | Closed 2026-08-10 | Ownership obligations; sema | Fixed where obligations are decided rather than by teaching the native backend to empty moved slots, which would make the nulling load-bearing and leave the wrong obligation in place. `arrays_drop_nested` matches its recorded answer on both backends. |
| RV2-DEBT-190 | A `select` over an unbuffered channel and a timer HANGS on the native backend at more than one worker. `testdata/golden/vm_async_suite/t22_select_wait_recv.sg` makes a capacity-0 channel, spawns a producer that sleeps and then sends, and selects between `ch.recv()` and `sleep(100).await()`. Built once and run repeatedly, the binary finishes in milliseconds and prints `v=7` almost always, and roughly one run in twenty never finishes at all: measured 0/20 hung at `SURGE_THREADS=1`, 1/20 at 2, 0/20 at 4, 1/20 at 8. A hung run produces no output and no error - it simply never exits, which is the signature of a wakeup that was posted and not observed rather than of a crash. Found 2026-08-10 by the multi-worker lane on its first run, which is the argument for that lane existing: the same fixture is byte-exact and stable at one worker, so every single-worker gate in the repository agrees it is fine. Worth noting how it first arrived, because the lane has since been changed to prevent a repeat: it presented as the whole `internal/vm` package timing out after an hour with the fixture name recoverable only from a goroutine dump. The lane now builds each fixture once and bounds each RUN at 20 seconds, so a hang is a named failure and the whole lane finishes in about four minutes. | Open | Runtime V2 native scheduler; select / unbuffered channel / timer interaction | The fixture completes on every run at every worker count the lane exercises. The suspected shape is a lost wakeup between a select arm's registration and a same-instant readiness - the producer's send and the select's park are the two sides - so the proof should be a deterministic sync-point reproduction rather than a repetition count, in the style of the existing cancelled-join-waiter control. A repetition count can only ever show the rate falling. |
